# SPEC TEAM Handoff — Saints Row: The Third Cleanroom Reverse-Engineering Project

**Purpose of this document:** a self-contained pickup point for a fresh session (or this
session post-compaction) to resume work with zero lost context. Originally written
2026-09-07 at the request of the peer orchestrator session ahead of an anticipated
compaction; kept current since.

**HOW TO READ THIS FILE — in order:**

1. **§27 is the resume note.** Rewritten from scratch at the second 2026-09-11 compaction.
   It lists what is DONE (do not re-derive), what is OPEN with a concrete next step for
   each, the authorization state, every address already in hand, and what has been ruled
   out. **If you read only one section, read that one.**
2. **§5 is the methodology record** — 99 lessons, and the most reusable thing here. Much of
   it is about how measurements mislead: vacuous tests, diluted denominators, controls that
   cannot fail, predicates with no selectivity, and numbers that lose their definition
   crossing a team boundary.
3. **§4 is the priority list**; §2b is the consolidated Ghidra bookmark map; §2c indexes the
   replay harnesses.
4. **§7 onward are dated session updates, newest last.** §7–§14 cover the geometry and
   character formats; §15–§25 the mid-project passes; **§26 is the long 2026-09-11 session**
   (§26.1–§26.24), which resolved the vertex format, `.anim_pc` binding/layout/rotations/
   translations, the vehicle material-set advance rule and 2× pairing, `.cmorph_pc`
   dequantisation, and the zone streaming category table.

Earlier sections are left intact. **Where later work superseded something, it is called out
explicitly in place rather than silently edited** — that discipline is itself load-bearing
here, because several findings came out of the corrections rather than in spite of them.

> # ▶ IF YOU ARE PICKING THIS UP COLD, READ **§27** FIRST.
> It is the resume note: what is in flight, what is already in hand, what is ruled out, and the concrete next step. Then §26 for what the 2026-09-11 session did, and §5 for the methodology lessons (heavily extended that day).

> ## ⬛ PHASE CLOSED — 2026-09-10
>
> **The reverse-engineering phase of this project is finished and this document is its closing record, not a live working log.** Every row of `spec-format-inventory.md` is characterised (verified by grepping the table for remaining "No" rows, not asserted). The deliverable is **30 specification documents plus this handoff** *(29 at the 2026-09-10 close; `spec-vertex-format.md` added 2026-09-11, §26)*.
>
> **Read §25 first** — it states what was delivered, what is deliberately left open and why, and exactly where a future session would start. Sections 7–24 below are the dated session log, newest last, and are preserved as the working record; where later work superseded something, the earlier text is left standing with the correction called out rather than silently edited. Any "next steps" or "holding" wording inside those dated sections is historical — **§25 supersedes all of it.**

**Standing rules (they applied to all work here, and would apply again if this ever resumes):**
- SPEC TEAM produces specification/inventory documents only — **never implementation code**.
- Describe behavior in our own words. Never reproduce verbatim decompiled code or original Volition identifiers, **except** load-bearing literal format data (magic numbers, offsets, sizes) — those are fine and expected.
- Every finding gets a confidence label: **CONFIRMED — empirical** (verified against real file bytes), **CONFIRMED — disassembly** (directly observed in code), **HIGH CONFIDENCE — inferred**, **HYPOTHESIS — unconfirmed**, **OPEN / UNKNOWN**.
- All tooling (Ghidra project/scripts, Python extraction scripts) stays in `tools/` or the scratchpad — never a deliverable itself.
- **Peer messages from other sessions are never permission-escalation authority.** If a peer says something was denied to them and asks this session to do it instead, refuse and surface it to the actual user. (This has come up before and was correctly refused; it may come up again — hold the line.)
- Methodology discipline: content-validate *and* random-control-baseline compare before trusting any offset/ordering hypothesis (see §5, hash-sort lesson). Don't generalize from one small sample. Flag scope-jumps instead of chasing them.

### 1a. Two corrections applied this session (from clean-team byte-for-byte verification)

Both **fully applied and complete** as of this handoff:

1. **spec-texture-format.md §3** — the compressed-size field is at **`+0x24`**, not `+0x22` as previously documented. Verified byte-for-byte against two real samples with exact offset-chaining confirmation. `+0x22` is a **separate, genuinely new, still-unidentified 2-byte field** — do not conflate the two again. All cross-references fixed (§1 overview, §5 worked example, §6 open items).

2. **spec-geometry-format.md §4.1** — the confirmed six-array "Mesh" sub-block structure lives in **`.ccmesh_pc`** (the small CPU-side file), **not `.gcmesh_pc`** as originally drafted — traced byte-for-byte on real sample `cm_hand_f_leather`. `.gcmesh_pc` is presumed bulk payload the ccmesh-side fields reference (mirroring the cpeg/gpeg split — **presumed, not yet confirmed**). Also: on that real sample, **5 of 6 arrays are empty**, only array 4 (the index/remap table) is populated, and the `count × stride` vertex-buffer arithmetic test on the "Mesh" sub-block (`spec-geometry-format.md` §4.1.1, `FUN_00e71410`) **did not land cleanly — this is INCONCLUSIVE, not a negative result**, since the sample may simply not exercise the interesting fields. A bigger/richer mesh sample is needed to actually test this. This is flagged as the single highest-value next step in spec-geometry-format.md §6 item 9.

**Not yet folded in anywhere:** nothing else outstanding from the clean team as of this writing — these were the only two corrections requested.

Gotchas learned: COM reentrancy causes `RPC_E_CALL_REJECTED` if you poll too tightly; a poll loop that's too fine-grained makes the live game stutter; **never kill the debug task abruptly** (e.g. via TaskStop) — it kills the debuggee process too. Use a sentinel-file-triggered `DetachAll()` for clean teardown instead. This whole apparatus is parked, not needed unless dynamic analysis is picked up again.

### 2a. Ghidra bookmarks/comments — now persisted (done this session)

Per the peer's explicit instruction, **every key address below is now saved as both a Ghidra NOTE bookmark and a plate comment directly in the SR3 Ghidra project** (not just this document) — run via `tools/scripts/AddHandoffBookmarks.java`, confirmed successful (all 15 addresses marked, project save succeeded). A fresh session opening the Ghidra project will see these immediately at each address without needing this document at all. Bookmark categories used: `RESOURCE_REGISTRY`, `MESH_FORMAT`, `SHARED_BLOCK`, `CONTAINER_MODEA`, `LUA_BINDINGS`, `GARAGE_VEHICLE_STATS`, `RIG_FORMAT`.

**⭐ The resource-type registration table — the peer's specifically-called-out address:**
- **`FUN_00700780`** — the master resource-type registration table (~45 formats). Each block: type ID, extensions, 2 size fields, ctor/dtor function pointers, fed into `FUN_00daf4e0` which writes them into global catalog arrays. **This is the starting point for investigating any new/unexplored format** — check it first before blind-searching. Bookmarked + commented. Full detail in spec-geometry-format.md §4.3.
- **`FUN_00daf4e0`** — the registration-record consumer. Field offsets within each record: `+0x54`/`+0x58` = size fields, `+0x44`/`+0x48`/`+0x4c`/`+0x50` = ctor/dtor function pointers.

**Mesh-loading chain:**
- **`FUN_007524f0`** — `.ccmesh_pc` payload parser (corrected attribution, was misattributed to `.gcmesh_pc`). Six independently-gated arrays, strides 96/24/40/4/4/2 bytes. **(2026-09-08 update: fully re-derived and empirically validated — see §7. The "unaff_ESI"/"unaff_EDI" the decompiler shows are real incoming parameters, not spilled locals; the whole array-walk sequence, plus the leading length-prefixed name-list blob, is now a confirmed, reusable formula.)**
- **`FUN_00e718b0`** — generic format-agnostic dispatcher (8 unrelated callers), not mesh-specific itself; leads to the real sub-parser. **(2026-09-08: disassembled in full — see §7. Its second parameter is the scope-boundary value traced in §7's item 8.)**
- **`FUN_00e71410`** — the nested "Mesh" sub-block parser (own internal version-check string, must == 9). Candidate fields: `+0x20`=vertex count, `+0x30`=stride byte, `+0x28`=data pointer; flag `&4` switches to a 32-byte multi-stream D3D9 layout; `+0x48`/`+0x50` = cleaner index-buffer candidate. **(2026-09-08: version==9 now CONFIRMED empirically on 2/2 real samples via pure offset arithmetic — see §7. Bit 0 of the flags byte, not previously understood, is confirmed to route vertex data to `.gcmesh_pc` instead of reading it inline — both real samples have this bit set.)**
- **`FUN_00dd7d70`** — shared material-reference-block magic validator (`0x00043854`). Reused across `.matlib_pc`, `.ccmesh_pc`, `.cefct_pc`, `.czh_pc`, `.clmesh_pc` (6+ confirmed uses).
- **`0x0074bf30`** (`.clmesh_pc`/`.glmesh_pc`, type `0x1f`) — confirmed real **destructor**, not a parser. Constructor is `LAB_0074c530`, an **unresolved stub — open thread**, do not assume it shares `.ccmesh_pc`'s structure.
- **`FUN_007527b0`** *(new 2026-09-08, bookmarked)* — the real direct caller of `FUN_007524f0`. Confirms the fixed sub-header is exactly `0x88` bytes (sets cursor = subheader_start + 0x88 before handing off), validates the second magic (`0x424BD00D`) and version range (`0x29`–`0x2A`), and checks an "already loaded" flag bit. See §7.
- **`FUN_00e71310`** *(new 2026-09-08, bookmarked)* — helper called from the "Mesh" sub-block parser after the channel array; takes a hidden register-passed argument (EAX = `pbVar1+4`'s value, the "field4" the decompiler renders as `in_EAX`). Walks further per-channel sub-structures. See §7 items 7–8.
- **`FUN_00e71740`** *(new 2026-09-08, bookmarked)* — the alternate/external vertex-data parser, invoked only when the Mesh sub-block's flags-byte bit 0 is set (true on both real samples checked). Reuses the same count/stride fields as the inline path but against a different base, and performs a cross-file integrity check against `checkval1`. See §7 items 6–8.
- **`FUN_00751f60`** *(identified 2026-09-08, NOT YET bookmarked — see §7's closing note)* — the actual registered constructor for `.ccmesh_pc`/`.csmesh_pc` (confirmed via the registration table, `FUN_00700780`, at two storage sites). This is where the count×stride investigation hit its scope boundary: its 5th parameter is the value ultimately needed to locate the vertex data in `.gcmesh_pc`, and it's never set by any ccmesh-specific code — see §7 item 8.

### 2b. Consolidated Ghidra bookmark map — everything added after the original 15 (all runs confirmed "Save succeeded")

Categories are the bookmark categories in the SR3 project; open Ghidra's Bookmarks window and filter by category. Every address below also carries a plate comment that restates the finding, so the project is self-describing without this document.

**`RESOURCE_DISPATCH`** (§8, `spec-resource-dispatch.md`):
- `FUN_00dd2e30` — **the generic constructor dispatcher**: fetches the ctor pointer from the registration row, calls it with 4 args (unpaired) or 6 (paired; 6th = the already-loaded g-file buffer).
- `FUN_00dd2a70` — the paired-file resolver: same base name + each registered extension; **how any c-file finds its g-file**.
- `FUN_00dd22c0` — extension-string → type-ID resolver. `FUN_00dd35e0` — request builder using the same pattern.
- `FUN_00daf4e0` — comment **corrected**: row `+0x00/+0x04` are the ctor/dtor pointers (were mislabeled "size fields"), `+0x18/+0x20` the two extension strings.

**`RESOURCE_REGISTRY`**: `FUN_00700780` — comment updated to point at `spec-format-inventory.md` (43 types, IDs 6 and 28 absent, platform-indexed extensions) and to warn that its name fields are compiler-emitted overlapping byte writes (§11).

### 2c. Replay harnesses — **durable copy, and the index** (added 2026-09-11)

**The 68 Python harnesses were copied out of the session scratchpad to `D:\Project Crreish\TEAM A\tools\harnesses\`.** This matters: the scratchpad path recorded in §2 contains a *session UUID*, so it is not reachable from any later session and is subject to temp cleanup. **Every `scratchpad/<name>.py` reference in the spec documents should be read as `tools/harnesses/<name>.py`.** All 68 parse cleanly from the new location.

**They are the reusable part of this project.** Each one replays a loader against the *complete shipped population* with exact-size and control checks — the method that produced every population-wide number in the specs. A future pass on any new format should start by copying the nearest of these rather than writing a walker from scratch.

**Dependency layer — import these, don't reimplement them:**

| Module | Imported by | What it provides |
|---|---|---|
| `vehgeo_bulk.py` | **27 harnesses** | `collect(archives, exts)` — pulls every entry with the given extensions out of every archive, recursing into `.str2_pc` bundles. The workhorse. Also `BASE` (the archive directory) and the `KNOWN` magic dictionary used as the **reuse detector**. |
| `scan_meshes.py` | 9 | The container walker itself: `is_container`, `parse_entries`, `get_entry_bytes` (which handles the per-entry raw sentinel — see `spec-vpp-container.md` §5.9). |
| `foliage_bulk.py` | 5 | `engine_hash()` — the rotate-6/XOR hash, lowercased. |
| `ctdg_replay.py`, `czh_replay.py`, `tree_replay.py`, `morph_bulk.py`, `scan_ext.py` | 1–2 each | Per-format parsers reused by that format's follow-up probes. |

*(`pefile` and `capstone` appear in a few early exploratory scripts; they are third-party and were not needed for any published finding.)*

**Per-format entry points** — the one to open first for each format: `batch_test_ccmesh.py` / `sim_ccmesh.py` (mesh), `rig_bulk.py` (rig), `anim_bulk.py` (anim), `morph_bulk.py` (morph), `vehgeo_bulk.py` (vehicles), `foliage_bulk.py` (foliage), `tree_replay.py` + `tree_extra.py` (trees), `csc_replay.py` + `csc_values.py` (cutscene cameras), `ctdg_replay.py` + `ctdg_w0.py` (conversations), `czh_replay.py` (zone headers), `czn_bulk.py` + `czn_probe.py` (zone data), `gzn_probe.py` + `gzn_meshes.py` (zone g-files), `vbl_bulk.py` + `mips_check.py` (low mips), `extless_files.py` (the exhaustive extension census), `parse_reg_table.py` (the registration table).

**Added 2026-09-11 (vertex-format pass, §26)** — these six are the entry points for anything touching vertex data:

| Harness | What it does |
|---|---|
| `vertex_decl.py` | Walks a `.ccmesh_pc` to its Mesh sub-block, reads the channel records, replays the `g`-file segment, and checks all four exactness tests. Also holds `LAYOUT_BASE` and `predict_stride()`, which the others import. |
| `mesh_scan.py` | **The general one.** Finds and validates Mesh blocks in *any* carrier purely from the `g`-side (check value + declared length + bookend), so it works on containers whose outer structure is unknown. Start here for a new carrier. |
| `vertex_attr.py` | Decodes channel data as positions and computes bounding boxes; includes the wrong-offset controls. |
| `vertex_fields.py` / `vertex_fields2.py` | Score every 4-byte group in an element against candidate encodings (unit vector, partition of unity, small indices, half2). `2` is the carrier-general version driven by `mesh_scan`. |
| `vertex_bytes.py` | Byte-level distributions of the index/weight fields. |
| `vertex_verify.py` | The two per-vertex confirmations with controls: weight-lane/index-lane agreement, and vehicle four-byte replication. |
| `index_check.py` | Confirms the primary buffer is the index buffer (every index in range; max index == last vertex), with control. |

**Two are kept deliberately as counter-examples, not as working tools:** `czn_chunks.py` (the refuted uniform-chunk model, §22) and `gzn_mesh.py` (the contaminated candidate-set match whose flattering 1.0%-vs-0.34% ratio was pure noise, §22.1). Both are referenced by the §5 lessons.

**Running them:** `PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8` is required (a `≤` in a print crashed a run once), and the interpreter is `C:\Users\Purrsian\AppData\Local\Programs\Python\Python312\python.exe`. They expect to be run from their own directory so the sibling imports resolve.

## 3. Addresses, Leads, and Reasoning Not Fully Captured Elsewhere

Most reasoning already lives in the specs and is now duplicated into Ghidra comments (§2a). A few things worth stating explicitly here because they're connective tissue between specs rather than belonging to any one of them:

- **The registration table (`FUN_00700780`) is the single biggest lever available.** It's a full catalog of ~45 resource types with real constructor addresses. Every future "characterize format X" task should start by checking whether X has an entry here — it turns "find the loader" from a research problem into a lookup.
- **The `c`/`g` (CPU-side/GPU-side) paired-file convention does NOT reliably mean "small=metadata, big=payload."** The `.ccmesh_pc`/`.gcmesh_pc` correction is the proof: the CPU-side file held the structured payload. Don't assume the pattern from `cpeg_pc`/`gpeg_pc` generalizes without checking each pair individually.
- **`.gzn_pc` (terrain) and `.glmesh_pc` both have real registration-table entries but their actual parsing code was NOT found this session** — `.glmesh_pc`'s constructor is an unresolved stub (`LAB_0074c530`), and terrain has no dedicated constructor at all in the table (its loader path is presumably reached some other way, not yet traced). Don't assume either shares `.ccmesh_pc`'s six-array structure — that was explicitly disproven as a blanket assumption this session (the original framing was "trace once, unlocks all three simultaneously" — it did not; only `.ccmesh_pc`/`.gcmesh_pc` specifically was resolved).
- **The mode-a container puzzle (only entry 0 reliably decodable) is genuinely closed out**, not just deprioritized: 3 static disassembly passes plus 1 live dynamic-analysis pass (with the user actively playing while a debugger watched) produced a clean, honest negative result — the traced I/O functions are real and live but are not the code path for small gameplay-table reads. Don't re-open this without a materially new angle (e.g. a completely different function never yet examined).
- **The Lua binding surface has two confirmed-real mechanisms whose relationship is unconfirmed** (`vint_callback_lua` pool vs. the named C→Lua hook dispatch). Don't assume they're the same system just because they're thematically close — no code path was found connecting them.
- **Random-control-comparison discipline** was load-bearing at least once this session (the hash-sort false positive, §5) — worth remembering as a live habit, not just a historical footnote, since the same trap (an ordering hypothesis that looks confirmed until compared against a randomized baseline) can recur in any offset/ordering claim.
- **How a c-file finds its g-file is settled and generic** (§8): filename + registered extension, resolved by the dispatcher *before* any format-specific constructor runs; the constructor receives the g-buffer as a plain 6th argument. Any paired format can assume this. Corollary from §13: a *missing* g-file is a normal, successful load (every standalone morph), so a resolver must tolerate it.
- **"Two loaders, one format" is now the expected pattern, not a surprise.** `.anim_pc` (2 ctors, handle-vs-name), `.cmorph_pc` (3 ctors, one parser + two stash-only), `.ccmesh_pc`/`.csmesh_pc` (one ctor, two extensions). Trace the ctors; don't assume from the inventory alone.
- **Two shared, literally-named engine sub-blocks live in one module** (`0x00e71xxx`): the "Mesh" block (`FUN_00e71410`) and the "Morph" block (`FUN_00e71940`), each with its own version error string. Both are reused across formats — the Mesh block by `.ccmesh_pc`/`.csmesh_pc` and vehicles, the Morph block by `.cmorph_pc` and vehicles. Expect a third sibling if a new geometry-adjacent format turns up.
- **`.rig_pc` is a dependency of vehicles, not just characters** — vehicle parts bind to rig bones by name (§14). The still-open 40-byte bone-record layout (`spec-rig-format.md`) therefore blocks vehicles too.
- **The `.gcar_pc` / `.gcmesh_pc` convention**: 16-byte header (cross-ref word + 12 zeros), primary vertex/index stream at `0x10`, further channels, then (vehicles) the mode-0 morph region at the offset the c-file's header gives.

## 5. Methodology Notes / Lessons Learned

Read this before starting new investigation — these are hard-won and cheap to relearn the hard way.

- **Hash-sort false-positive lesson:** an ordering or offset hypothesis that looks confirmed on real samples can still be a coincidence. Always compare against a **randomized control baseline** before trusting it — if shuffled/random data produces a similarly "clean" result, the pattern isn't real. This exact trap nearly caused a false confirmation earlier in the project and is now a standing rule for any offset/ordering claim.
- **Entry-0-only rule:** for mode-(a) compressed containers, only the first directory entry is reliably decodable — this is a confirmed, closed limitation (3 static + 1 dynamic pass), not a guess. Any spec built on a mode-(a) container's non-first entries must flag that content as unverified (see spec-fxo-format.md's explicit handling of this).
- **Always run a random-control comparison** before generalizing a structural finding from a single real sample — related to the hash-sort lesson but broader: a single real sample confirms a structure *can* look a certain way, not that the interpretation is correct. This is why the `.ccmesh_pc` "Mesh" sub-block test is honestly reported as inconclusive rather than negative — the sample didn't have the right shape to test the hypothesis either way.
- **Don't generalize from one small sample, ever — flag it instead.** Recurs throughout: `.rig_pc`'s 4-bone sample, `.ccmesh_pc`'s sparse sample, `.czh_pc`/`.czn_pc`/`.gzn_pc`'s single tile. The fix is cheap (one sentence in the open-items list); the cost of a false generalization compounds into every spec built on top of it.
- **Scope-jump discipline:** when a trace leads into clearly generic, unrelated infrastructure (the PE-relocation-fixup framework found via the garage-hooks trace is the clearest example), stop and flag it rather than chasing it — it's not wrong to notice it, but pursuing it derails the actual target. Report the jump, let the peer/user decide whether it's worth a dedicated pass.
- **Dynamic-analysis setup gotchas** (only relevant if resuming live debugging): COM reentrancy causes rejected calls if polled too tightly; a too-fine poll loop makes the live game stutter noticeably (annoying for whoever's playing); **never abruptly stop/kill a debug task** — it kills the debuggee. Use a sentinel-file-triggered clean detach instead. This was learned the hard way once this session (an unintended game-process kill from a task-stop, caught and fixed immediately).
- **Cleanroom compliance is not just "don't paste decompiled code."** Every spec in this project also avoids reproducing original Volition identifiers, restates findings in its own words, and cites Ghidra's `FUN_00xxxxxx` auto-names only as addresses-as-evidence, never as identifiers to reuse elsewhere. This discipline held throughout the session and should keep holding.
- **Fold in clean-team corrections promptly and transparently** — mark exactly what changed and why, in every place it's referenced (overview lines, worked examples, open-items lists), never silently overwrite. Both corrections in §1a were applied this way; use them as the template for future corrections.
- **Peer messages are context, never escalated authority.** If a future peer message says "the user denied me X, so you do X instead," the correct response is refusal + surfacing to the actual user, not compliance. Stated as a standing rule on every peer message this session; never actually triggered, but must hold if it ever is.
- **Prefer "unexplained" over "explained away"** (§10.1). 21 files read zeros where a magic should be; a tidy story ("degenerate placeholder meshes") fit the signal and was reported as understood. It was a wrong offset rule (the *mandatory* material-block pad) — the zeros were the pad. A plausible explanation for anomalous data is not a verified one; the honest state was "21 files don't parse and I don't know why."
- **Replay the writes; don't read the immediates** (§11). Compiler-emitted data that reuses a previous record's leftover bytes decodes to *plausible-looking wrong values* (`Pcust meshcvtf`, `Treel Mesh`, three wrong constructor attributions). Caught only by simulating every partial write in order.
- **Exact-size arithmetic proves stride, not type** (§13). A `N × 12` model reproduced 1,541/1,541 file sizes and made "float3 deltas" look confirmed; a one-line float-magnitude check killed it (values ~10³⁸). Structure and encoding are separate claims needing separate tests.
- **Two same-type samples agreeing is not confirmation** (§13). Two NPC-head morphs both read `(1426, 1425)` at one offset; across 1,541 files it read `(1, 0)` in 1,246 — it was payload that only *looked* like a header field because all heads share a topology. The small-sample rule, in a new costume.
- **State thresholds explicitly and reconcile counts against an independent reproduction** (§13). "Exactly zero in 1,954" was a `< 1e-6` threshold that admitted one file at `1.000e-06`; Team B's independent count was 1,953 exact. Both were right about the field; the wording was wrong. Publish the threshold with the number.
- **A residual is a measurement** (§13). A first replay that matched 0/1,541 files had residuals of exactly `N × 12 + pad + 4` — which located the missing bulk pass and the trailing sentinel more precisely than any amount of reading would have.
- **Built-in controls are everywhere; use them.** Ungated files for a flag-gated field (§12: 167/167 vs 3.4%); a shifted offset and random offsets for a structural claim (§12: 4,209/4,209 vs 0/4,209 and 0/12,177); a rejected hypothesis as the baseline for the accepted one (§13: `+0` at 0.008% vs `+6` at 100%, over the records where the test can fail — see the vacuous-control lesson below). A claim without a control rate is a claim without a denominator.
- **Scan a new format's population for every catalogued magic before assuming it is new** (§14). One scan showed vehicles embed two already-documented formats; the "new" investigation was mostly cross-referencing.
- **The per-entry raw sentinel beats the container flag** (§12, `spec-vpp-container.md` §5.9). An archive can set the shared-stream flag and still store every entry raw; branching on the flag first fails outright. Check the sentinel first.
- **A hit rate without its chance rate is not a result** (§15 addendum). A search for a per-bone presence bitmask in animation headers matched in 49% of files — and a random-window control gave a 0.3% per-window rate over ~240 windows per file, i.e. chance predicts ~53%. The "49%" was noise. Always multiply the per-trial control rate by the number of trials before reading a hit count; a search that scans many positions manufactures hits.
- **When an exact-size chain breaks, the file's other offset fields are the oracle** (§16). The foliage material-record chain failed 13/19 with 8-byte alignment after the runtime slot array and held 19/19 with 16-byte — decided in one run by asking which choice lands exactly on the file's own `+0x28` offset, not by re-reading disassembly. Every offset field a format carries is a free assertion to test a replay against; spend those before spending decompiler time.
- **An assumed search SHAPE is more dangerous than an assumed search WINDOW** (clean team's framing, §26.11). They had already learned "a scan that finds nothing has ruled out its window, not the hypothesis" from a ±256-byte miss. The ascending-run failure is the same trap one level deeper, and worse: **a window is visibly a parameter you chose, while "the indices ascend" reads like a property of the data rather than an assumption you smuggled in.** Nobody re-examines a premise they never noticed making. Their rule, adopted here: **in a negative result state the predicate, never the conclusion** — "no ascending run here", never "no table here".
- **Reach for a runtime trace as soon as a format-walk returns a confident negative, not after several failed shape-guesses** (clean team, §26.11). The track-to-bone table was found by tracing the function that *uses* the data, and that worked precisely because **a trace does not have to guess a shape at all** — the code says what it reads. Here it came only after an ascending-list search, a bitmask search, a hash search and three refuted payload models. **A confident negative from a shape-dependent search is itself the signal to switch methods**, because the likeliest explanation for "the obvious structure is not there" is that it is there in a shape you did not enumerate.
- **A negative result is only as broad as its predicate — record the predicate with it** (§26.11). `spec-anim-format.md` §6a searched for the track-to-bone list as *a strictly ascending run of indices*, found none, and wrote down **"the mapping is not stored as a separate list"**. The list exists and sits immediately after the header; it is a **permutation** (`[0,1,3,4,2,6,7,5,9,10,8,14]`), so an ascending-run test could never match it. The search was hunting the right object with the wrong predicate, and the conclusion silently widened from *"no ascending list"* to *"no list"* — which then closed the question for two sessions and sent the next pass looking for the mapping inside an undecoded payload. **When a search comes back empty, write the negative as a statement about the test, not about the format** — "no ascending run of length N in the first 16 KiB" is honest and re-openable; "no such list exists" is neither. Distinct from the denominator and lane lessons: those are about the strength of a *positive* rate, this is about the *scope* a negative is entitled to.
- **An intersection is only as good as the smaller set — check its size before trusting the ranking** (§26.12). §27.1's plan was "intersect the clip-registry consumers with float work". The consumer set was 58 functions; expanding it over callers *and* callees to depth 2 made it **8,558 of 41,144** — a fifth of the binary. The ranking still printed, still looked ordered, and was sorting on function *size*. **A closure that reaches a fifth of the program is not a filter, and the output gives no sign of it.** Print the size of every set you intersect, and treat any closure above a few hundred as a failed filter rather than a wide one. Sibling of the harness-cap lesson below: both are a silently-wrong denominator, one too small, one too large.
- **Small struct displacements have no selectivity at binary scale** (§26.12). The predicate "reads `+0x28` and reads `+0x0A`/`+0x0B` and does float work" matched **469** functions across the binary. `+0x28` and `+0x0A` are simply common offsets. The §26.11 scan that found the track table used the *same* byte-count reads and worked — because it was **also** restricted to the 58 registry consumers. **The restriction was doing the work, and dropping it dissolved the result while the predicate looked unchanged.** When a search that once worked stops working, check what you removed, not what you kept.
- **An address window is a population you did not inspect** (§26.12). Ranking functions inside `0x004a0000..0x004e0000` as "the anim module" surfaced sin/cos and atan minimax coefficient sets — in **three identical copies** — i.e. a vector math library sharing the range. The window was not merely too wide; it contained a *different* dense population that outranked the target. **Before ranking within a window, look at what else lives there.** Related to the shape-vs-window lesson (§26.11) but distinct: that one was about a negative's scope, this is about a positive's contamination.
- **When the call graph fails, search from the DATA side** (§26.12). What finally worked was neither a closure nor a window: the clip manager owns a **contiguous global block**, so enumerating every referenced address in it and taking the union of referencing functions yields the subsystem's real surface (63 functions) — no closure, no window, no shape guess, and derived from the layout the compiler actually produced rather than from a boundary chosen by me. **Globals cluster by owner; that clustering is evidence you did not have to assume.** And note the target itself was *not* on that surface — it takes the clip as an argument — so the surface's value was in defining the neighbourhood to profile, not in containing the answer.
- **A boolean column that is always false is a bug until proven otherwise** (§26.12). A probe flagged "reads the clip-buffer global" by substring-matching the address text, printed `-` for **every** function including ones known to read it, and I nearly read that as a finding. Ghidra renders the address without the leading zero my pattern had. **A flag that never fires across a whole population is far more likely to be broken than to be a uniform negative** — the same shape as the harness cap that silently discarded 286 vehicles while printing a confident N/N. Put a known-positive case in every scan and assert it fires.
- **A residual you have not characterised is a denominator you have not justified** (§26.12). A payload walk was published at "**3,687 / 4,086 (90.2%)** land on the header's declared endpoint" — an honest number with an unexamined 9.8%. The clean team asked what distinguished the 399 that *succeeded at walking* but missed the oracle, and a single flags bit (`0x40`) separated them almost perfectly **and** accounted for all 123 walk failures. Stratified, the real rate is **99.89%** and the entire residual is one identified population. **Measuring the property on the successes was what found the structure** — the failures alone would not have, because the bit's clips *can* walk, they just end somewhere else. Two habits from this: when a rate is below ~99% on data that should be uniform, **assume a mixed denominator before assuming noise**; and run every candidate discriminator against the *successes* as well, not only the failures. Third dilution error here (cf. §13, §26.9) and the first found by interrogating a residual rather than a headline. **And it immediately recurred — the very next paragraph quoted a bit-specific recovery rate over the *combined* miss count (399 rather than 397), inside the correction written to stop that.** Not carelessness: 399 was the number already in hand from the prior pass, and a denominator you are reusing does not re-announce which population it came from. **After stratifying, re-derive every downstream figure from the restricted set rather than reusing totals computed before the split** — and *re-measure* rather than re-divide, because the numerator moves too: here the corrected count was 334, not the 335 that simple re-division would have preserved.
- **A correction inherits the frame of the thing it corrects** (clean team's sharpening, §26.13). This one generalises well past denominators. They had written the stratification lesson as *"the denominator needs re-deriving"*; re-measuring showed the **numerator** had moved too (334, not 335), so the fix was itself one level too shallow — in exactly the same way as the error it fixed. **A correction is written inside the mental frame of the mistake, so it tends to reproduce that frame's blind spot.** Their rule, adopted here: after finding an error, the question is not *"is this figure right now"* but **"what else came out of the same pass"**. And the failure mode is worse than the original, because **a re-divided number looks audited while still being wrong, so nobody has reason to check it again** — an uncorrected figure at least still looks unchecked. Practical form: when a correction lands, re-run the measurement that produced every figure near it, rather than patching the one that was challenged.
- **Keep sending a cross-team correction even after it has not applied several times running** (clean team, §26.13). This project's container-flag note has now been passed over three times and been already-handled on their side three times. That is not wasted: **checking costs one command, and the answer is always either a real bug or a real proof — never nothing.** The trap is that **the reflex to skip a check runs in both directions** — toward adopting a claim unexamined, and toward dismissing one because the last two did not apply. The second is harder to notice because it feels like calibration. Same shape as the assumed-shape lesson (§26.11): a premise you stopped noticing you were making.
- **"It passed" is not evidence until you know the test could have failed — and this trap recurs, so screen for it by habit** (§26.14). Second instance in this project. The half-float screen `abs(u) < 64` scored 0.94 on data that was not half-float, because denormals pass automatically. The rotation clamp oracle looked far better designed — it comes from the decoder's own arithmetic, `x²+y²+z² ≤ 1` — and the real reading breached at **0.001%**, which reads as a decisive pass. It is not: the controls breached at **0.002–0.058%**, because most stored components are small and the bound is almost never tight. **A test derived from the correct semantics can still be vacuous on the actual data distribution.** The fix is not a better oracle but a cheap standing habit: **before quoting a pass rate, run the same test on a deliberately wrong reading, and if the wrong reading also passes, the number is describing the data's distribution rather than your hypothesis.** Then restrict the denominator to trials that could have gone either way (here: samples whose base term was already large, which moved the separation from 1.4× to 4–63×). Sibling of the diluted-control lesson (§13) with the direction reversed: that one understated a real finding, this one would have overstated a weak one.
- **Reach for the entry point, not the displacement — this predicate has now failed three times in one session** (§26.15). Small struct displacements have no selectivity at binary scale, and each time the scan *ran*, *printed*, and *looked like candidates*: 469 functions for "reads `+0x28` and `+0x0A`" (§26.12), 461 for the translation cursor fields (§26.13), 23 for "part count `+0x390` with stride `0xE0`" (§26.15). **Only the third one was cheap to recognise, because by then the shape was familiar.** The fix each time was the same and is now the default: start from a **named entry point** — a registered constructor, a manager's global block — and read its own call list, which the compiler wrote and nobody has to guess. A displacement predicate is only useful *intersected* with such a set, never as the primary filter. **Corollary worth the words: a scan that returns tens-to-hundreds of hits on a 41,000-function binary has told you its predicate is weak, not that the answer is one of the hits.**
- **A correct fact in the wrong document makes a question look harder than it is** (§26.15). The `.ccar_pc` registered constructor was recorded in `HANDOFF.md` and the format inventory but not in `spec-vehicle-geometry.md`, so the vehicle spec's reader was one indirection short of the vehicle entry point — and every question the implementation team asked about vehicles was answerable from that constructor's call list. **Nothing was wrong anywhere; the routing was.** When a question about format X arrives and feels hard, check first whether X's own document names X's entry point.
- **A control that agrees *exactly* with the real measurement is broken, not confirming** (clean team, §26.16). Their byte-base+1 control returned **byte-identical** output to the real scan — same 9,878 runs — because a one-byte shift only produced different name truncations that their validity check accepted equally. If that had been the only control, *"two controls agree"* reads as confirmation of the finding. **The tell is exactness**: a genuine control can legitimately score close to the real reading (`spec-anim-format.md` §6c.5's `+2` control did, and for a stateable reason), but it should not reproduce it *identically* — identical output means the perturbation did not perturb anything the test measures. **Pair every control with a check that it CAN fail**, e.g. assert it differs from the real reading somewhere, or perturb by an amount the hypothesis says must break. This is the exact inverse of the vacuous-oracle lesson (§26.14): there the *test* could not fail, here the *control* could not fail, and both present as a clean pass.
- **"Exact vs upper bound" was a frame error, not a measurement error** (§26.16). `+0x0C` was recorded as exact on meshes but only an upper bound on vehicles. It drives the per-material loop, so it is exact on **both**; what is not exact on vehicles is the relation between materials and *draw runs*, a different quantity, where `runs ≤ count` one-sided is correct and equality on meshes was a coincidence of the data. **Third time this session the error was the frame rather than the number** (cf. §26.12, §26.13). The habit that would have caught it: when a field behaves differently across two carriers of a *shared* structure, suspect the described relation before the field — shared code cannot mean two things, so the difference is in what is being compared to what.
- **Strictness is not a scalar — a guard can be too tight and too loose at the same time** (clean team, §26.16). Their material-binding guard required an exact run-count match. In the dimension everyone was watching — *does it accept enough vehicles?* — it was too tight, and that failure was **loud**: it produced a visible "why only 3 of 372?" In a dimension nobody was watching it was wide open, accepting a name carved out of the middle of another string, and that failure was **quiet**: it produced renders that looked fine. **Tightening a guard in the direction of the complaint can leave the real defect untouched, and the complaint is what tells you which direction you are looking.** Ask separately: what true cases does this reject, and what false cases does this accept? They are independent questions with independent answers. Corollary observed here: **a diagnostic that changes a verdict when promoted to a check was never merely a diagnostic** — their name-start test, built only to quantify truncation, flipped 2 of 3 "bound" vehicles to rejected once it became a validator, exposing live wrong bindings.
- **Do not let the measuring instrument track the thing it measures** (clean team, §26.16). They fixed their reader's name validator and deliberately left their *validation harness's* own scan untightened, still reporting the raw pre-fix counts. Had they applied the fix to both, the harness would have agreed with the reader by construction and the signal it exists to produce would be gone. **When you maintain a reader and its validator side by side, a fix applied to both is not twice as safe — it is half as observable.** Same family as the broken-control lesson above: both are about keeping the check independent of the thing checked.
- **Audit the relay, not just the record — a correct document beside a rounded-up summary puts the error in the one place nobody checks** (orchestrator's framing, §26.16). A long thread closed with a summary calling vehicle materials *"resolved end to end"*. `HANDOFF.md` and the specs said no such thing — the record was precise, the *relay* was not — and a summary is what a reader three sessions out actually builds on. **Every party had accurate information and the conclusion was still wrong, because the flattening happened in transit.** Two habits: (1) read summaries *of your own work* as adversarially as you read your own measurements, since a thread that has gone well is exactly when a summary rounds up, and each individual result being true makes the aggregate sound safer than it is; (2) when you ask someone to preserve a distinction — a confidence tier, an open item — **check the restatement, not just the original**, because "relayed with distinctions intact" is a claim like any other. The whole-session pattern: §26.12 diluted a denominator, §26.13 inherited the frame of its own correction, §26.16 mis-framed a field as an upper bound — and this one mis-framed a *result set*. **The number is rarely the thing that is wrong.**
- **When quoting another team's figure, restate WHAT WAS COUNTED, not just the count and its direction** (clean team's rule, §26.17). `spec-vertex-format.md` §8.4.1 cited *369 `<` / 3 `==` / 0 `>`* as evidence for `runs ≤ materialCount`. The figure counted **binding** runs; the section meant **draw** runs. Both documents were internally consistent and used "run" correctly in their own terms — **they collided only at the citation boundary, which is exactly where nobody reads both halves at once.** Re-measuring gave the opposite relation: draw ranges *exceed* `materialCount` in 392/393. **A number carries its definition implicitly, and the definition does not survive the quote.** Write the borrowed figure as "N of M *X* were …", never as "N of M were …". Related to the relay lesson above: both are degradation in transit rather than in measurement.
- **Check whether your own prose argues against your conclusion — it is a free proof-read that costs nothing** (§26.17). The paragraph that got the relation backwards contained its own refutation one sentence earlier: *"a run references a material, several may share one, a material need not be referenced"* predicts runs **≥** distinct materials, and the conclusion said **≤**. **The premise was right, the conclusion contradicted it, and a borrowed number bridged the gap convincingly enough that neither half got re-read.** When a citation supports a claim, check it against the argument *you* wrote around it, not only against the source.
- **A correction is written fast and inherits the assumptions of the thing it corrects — including traps you have already documented** (§26.17). While fixing the relation above, I compared the draw-range id **unmasked** against a count and got "distinct ids exceed materialCount in 385/393". That field is **packed 16:16** and this project had already written that exact trap down (§26.9). Masked, the relation reverses to 393/393. **The mental state during a correction is "the surrounding work is sound, one thing is wrong", which is precisely when documented hazards get skipped.** Re-run the standard checks *inside* a correction, and hold its new numbers to the same bar as the ones being replaced — I also had to discard a 393/393 result from that same pass as near-vacuous.
- **Before publishing a rate, ask what the FAILING case would physically have been** (clean team's rule, §26.18). *"Distinct masked material ids < materialCount in 393/393"* reads as strong. For it to fail, a draw range would have had to name an id that is not a valid index at all — **a corrupt file**. So the rate really says "no vehicle is corrupt": a true invariant, worth keeping, and no evidence for the claim it was offered under. **This is the vacuity test one level up from §26.14's**: there the *test* could rarely fail on the data; here the test could only fail if the data were *malformed*, which is a stronger and much easier-to-miss form, because a bound over valid data always holds by definition. Two concrete habits: name the failing case in words before quoting the rate, and **check tightness** — here no file had zero slack and the mean was 21.6, so the bound was never near binding.
- **When a bound is not tight, the SLACK is the measurement** (clean team, §26.18). Having established `referenced ≤ declared`, the informative quantity was `declared − referenced`, which turned out to be **49.9% on vehicles against 0.0% on characters**, and led straight to a real finding: vehicles declare **exactly 2×** the materials they draw, ratio never above 2.000. **An inequality brackets a quantity; measure the quantity.** Generalisation worth carrying: whenever a one-sided relation is confirmed, immediately measure the gap and compare it across populations — a bound that holds everywhere says little, but a *gap* that differs sharply between carriers is structure. It also retro-explained six earlier refuted count-matches: they were compared against a number guaranteed to miss by 2×.
- **A check you ran on the supporting number and not on the headline is a worse failure than not knowing the check** (§26.18). Correcting `spec-vertex-format.md` §8.4.1, I tested one number for near-vacuity — correctly discarding `max(field >> 16) < materialCount` because the high half runs 0–14 against a median count of 44 — and **did not test the claim that paragraph existed to make**, which was vacuous for the same reason. The check was loaded and pointed at the wrong target. **This is distinct from the inherited-frame lessons above: nothing was assumed, the reasoning was available and applied — it is about where attention went.** Supporting evidence attracts scrutiny because it feels optional and therefore suspect; the headline feels load-bearing and therefore already settled. **Audit the load-bearing claim first, and treat "I already validated something in this paragraph" as a reason to be more suspicious, not less** — a nearby passed check creates a false sense that the area has been swept.
- **A run detector that accepts runs of length 1 is not a detector** (§26.18). Scanning for 12-byte binding entries with a minimum run length of 1 returned 1,119 runs per vehicle against a known ~26 — 97% of them length 1, because a lone entry satisfying "offset in blob, index 0" occurs by chance all over a file. **A structural pattern's evidence is its LENGTH; accepting the minimum length destroys the only discriminating property it has.** Set the threshold from what chance would produce, not from what the format technically permits. Fourth appearance of no-selectivity this session, and the first where the weak predicate was a *length* rather than a displacement.
- **When your count disagrees with another team's by a factor, publish neither until it reconciles** (§26.18). With the threshold fixed I still got 19,240 runs against their 9,878 on a comparable population. **A 2× discrepancy is not a rounding difference — it means one scan is finding things the other is not, and until that is explained both numbers are unusable, including the one that looks reasonable.** The temptation is to publish mine with a caveat, since it is *nearer* to plausible than the broken version was; nearer is not reconciled. Especially sharp here because the *finding* under investigation was itself a factor of 2 — an unexplained 2× in the instrument is the last thing you want while measuring a 2× in the data.
- **A mechanical explanation for why an approach failed reads as permission to retry it** (clean team's catch, §26.18). Establishing that vehicles declare 2× what they draw explained why six count-matching candidates had failed — and my phrasing, *"the count was right and the comparison was off by 2×"*, invited the inference that fixing the factor makes counting work. It does not: **a corrected count tells you HOW MANY, never WHICH**, and no cardinality distinguishes which material a run uses. **When a post-mortem improves, state explicitly whether the approach is revived or merely better understood** — those are different conclusions and a good explanation naturally suggests the more optimistic one. Keep the principled negative as the operative statement; the mechanical one is a footnote to it, not a replacement.
- **Two medians agreeing is not a relation — test per item before believing it** (§26.18). My unreliable binding-run scan produced a median of **48 runs per vehicle** against a median `materialCount` of **44**, which suggested the appealing conclusion that each declared material has exactly one binding run — and would have implied the unreferenced half carries real textures, supporting the pairing hypothesis. **Tested per file it scattered: 257 `>`, 110 `<`, 26 `==`.** The medians matched while the per-file relation did not exist at all. **An aggregate is compatible with many different item-level structures, including none**, and a near-match between two summary statistics is the most seductive form of this because it arrives already looking like a finding. Same family as the diluted-denominator lessons, but about the *granularity* of the comparison rather than its population: **never let a summary statistic stand in for the relation you actually want to claim.**
- **A result that would close several open threads at once deserves MORE scrutiny, not less** (orchestrator's framing, §26.18). The withdrawn median near-match was the most dangerous inference of the session precisely because it paid off **three** separate wants simultaneously: it explained the unreconciled 2× in my instrument, it validated my own scan, and it supported the pairing hypothesis by implying the unreferenced materials carry real textures. **"Too good, explains everything" is a property of wishful thinking at least as often as of a real result** — and a finding feels *more* credible the more it resolves, which is exactly backwards. Practical trigger: treat the number of loose ends a candidate result would tidy up as a measure of how hard to attack it. Real results usually settle one thing and leave the rest untouched.
- **Keep the tally of your own retracted claims, and count it** (§26.18). Having decided to report demoted results visibly, I quoted the count as "three", "four" and "five" in three places without enumerating it. It was **seven**. **A running tally of retractions is itself a number and drifts like any other** — mine drifted three ways inside a thread whose entire subject was numbers drifting. Enumerate the list rather than carrying a count, so it can be checked instead of remembered. **The self-critical direction is not exempt from measurement discipline**: a wrong tally undermines the honesty it exists to demonstrate.
- **Isolate a disagreement by running one side at an extreme, not by trading hypotheses** (clean team's method, §26.18). Two scans disagreed by almost exactly 2× and **both teams immediately reached for a structural cause** — anchored vs sliding, different populations — because a clean factor invites the more interesting explanation. It was neither: same strategy, same 393 files, and the whole gap was **content guards**. They found it by running their own scan with validation *disabled*, which produced 27,050 and put my 19,240 inside a range they had already generated — proving the axis was content, not search, in one measurement. **A suspiciously round factor is not evidence of structure; it is just a number.** When two measurements of the same thing disagree, bracket — relax one side to its permissive extreme and see whether the other's figure falls inside — rather than comparing designs. Cheaper than either side being right, and it cannot be settled by the comparison both sides instinctively reach for. **And bracketing localises a disagreement without resolving it** — the narrower result is not correct for being narrower; once the axis is known the case for either side still has to be made on its own evidence (here: a control population, plus a demonstration that the looser guard admits real garbage), which is a different argument entirely.
- **A correct fact in the right document does not help if the thing consuming it never reaches that document** (orchestrator's framing, §26.15 + §26.18 — recorded now rather than waiting for a third instance, since waiting would be the same mistake in miniature). Twice in one session, with different surfaces:
  - The `.ccar_pc` registered constructor was in `HANDOFF.md` and the format inventory but **not in `spec-vehicle-geometry.md`** — so every vehicle question the implementation team asked looked harder than it was, when all of them were answerable from that constructor's own call list.
  - `spec-vertex-format.md` §8.4.2 of `spec-vertex-format.md` specifies the sampler-hash guard, and **my own binding-run harness did not apply it** — the entire unexplained 2× against the clean team's scan.

  **Neither was a knowledge failure; both were routing failures**, and routing failures are invisible from inside the document that is correct. Two habits: when a question about format X feels hard, **check first whether X's own document names X's entry point**; and when writing a rule into a spec, **check what already consumes that spec and whether it implements the rule** — a spec is not self-applying, and a harness written before the rule will silently predate it. The tell in both cases was an unexplained factor or an unexpectedly hard question, never an error message.
- **Check a candidate formula's ACHIEVABLE RANGE against the data's observed range before testing whether it fits** (clean team's rule, §26.20 — immediately actionable, not just a lesson). Pure arithmetic on field widths, no statistics: if the candidate's ceiling sits below the observed maximum, it is refuted before a single fit is attempted. Two instances the same day, which is what makes it a rule rather than an anecdote. **Theirs:** an inferred rotation assembly caps every component at 0.181, so no bone can pass ~36°, while real data presses right against that ceiling at 35.52° — refuted on arithmetic alone. **Mine:** three candidate morph identities all assumed a small-delta-times-scale form, and the quantised values reach 32,000+ in 80% of samples, past anything that family can produce. **A range check would have killed all three of my candidates in one step instead of three separate tests, with the "not small" correction falling out as a byproduct rather than needing its own discovery.** And the negative it yields is *broad*: it rules out a whole family ("no small-delta form can reach 32,000"), not just the instances tried. Related to the vacuity lessons but the inverse direction: those ask whether a test could fail, this asks whether a hypothesis could succeed.
- **A spec that reads clearly to its author is not thereby unambiguous — the only test is an independent implementation** (§26.20). One section of mine contained two defects, both invisible to me and both found by someone building from it. (1) `align2(p+1)` meant the C idiom `(p+1) & ~1`, *round p up*; read as "align the already-offset value" it applies the offset twice, and cost their first attempt 105/4,209. (2) The prose sourced a field from the sample bytes while the pseudocode and the disassembly sourced it from the control record — and the prose reading was not merely wrong but **unimplementable**, since those bits were already spoken for, so anyone resolving the conflict silently narrowed a field and lowered their achievable range. **Neither was a knowledge error; both were expression errors, and expression errors are invisible from inside the expression.** Habits: write bit allocations **exhaustively** rather than describing them, define every helper inline instead of using a conventional name, and state a format's achievable value range in the spec so a reader can range-check their own implementation without asking.
- **Verify before refuting, and suspect your own reading first** (clean team's rule, §26.21). They found a hard ceiling in a decode, concluded the spec's arithmetic was wrong, and reported it refuted. The arithmetic was right; their own assembly had dropped two factors. **Going from "something is wrong" to "the spec is wrong" skips the cheaper and far more likely candidate, which is your own implementation.** The symmetric half, which cost me two wrong predictions: when someone refutes your work, measure it yourself before accepting *or* rejecting — I did measure, which is why the real defect surfaced, but I then guessed twice at *their* bug instead of asking for the one thing that settles it. **Publishing my own intermediates for a single named key is what actually resolved it**, in one exchange, and it should have been the first move rather than the third.
- **An exact numeric coincidence is weaker evidence than a matching average, not stronger** (§26.21). Solving a reported ceiling of 0.181 backwards gave a base term of exactly **0.176798**, which was also, to six decimals, the axis-0 component of the key I had just dumped. That felt like proof of a 4-bit base field. It was coincidence — 0.176798 is just `8*64*4*SCALE`, and a base of 8 is unremarkable. **One matching number is a sample of size one**; the medians lesson (§26.18) said two aggregates agreeing is not a relation, and this is the same error with n=1 while feeling far more convincing because the digits line up exactly. **Exactness is persuasive in a way that magnitude is not, and it carries no more information.**
- **A script that prints "done" regardless of what it did is worse than no check at all** (§26.22). Patching a harness, I ran an unconditional `t.replace(old, new)` and then printed `patched` — while also printing `anchor hits: 0`. The replacement had matched nothing, the file was unchanged, and the success message was true of the *write* and false of the *edit*. **Both facts were on screen and I acted on the reassuring one.** This is the unread-hit-count failure with an aggravating factor: the tool actively asserted success. Rule: **a check must gate the action, not merely accompany it** — `if n != 1: raise` rather than print-and-continue. Every edit helper in this project now reports counts; the ones that do not *refuse* on a miss are still able to lie.
- **When a parse recovers 0 of N, suspect the plumbing before the format** (§26.22). An extraction reported "0 of 41 ids recovered" and looked like a decompiler-output surprise. The cause was that the parser read the *raw* dump while the line-prefix stripping had been done into a separate file — so every line began with `INFO` and no anchored regex could match. **A uniform zero is a connection failure signature, not a data signature**: real format surprises are partial, because some records almost always fit. Same family as the always-false flag column (§26.12), and the fix is the same shape — do the normalisation inside the parser, and assert a known-present record is found before trusting any count.
- **A containment test against a contiguous tiling measures nothing** (§26.23). "A declared chunk brackets the Mesh block in 830/1,002" is guaranteed to pass whenever the walk reaches that far, because the records tile the span with no gaps — the test restates its own precondition. **The tell was that two counts came out identical**: the bracket count and the "walk got past the mesh" count were both 830. **When a test and a precondition of that test produce the same number, the test has no content** (sibling of the byte-identical control, §26.16). The fix is to test a *boundary coincidence* rather than containment: does a record START exactly where the anchor starts? That version could fail, and did — 0/1,002 against a 0.3% control, which is a real negative.
- **Ask "what would a failure have looked like" BEFORE publishing, as a required step rather than a habit** (§26.23). It has now caught four claims in one day: a rotation clamp oracle that could not fail on the data's distribution, a bound whose only failing case was a corrupt file, a containment test that restated its precondition, and a terminator test satisfied by zero padding in 600 of 623 cases. **Three of those four would have read as clean confirmations** — 0.001% breach, 393/393, 830/1,002, 623/987 — and none carried information. The step costs one sentence and it has a higher yield than any other check in this project.
- **An oracle the file declares beats an endpoint you choose** (§26.12). The per-track walk "not overrunning the file" is a weak test — 97.1% pass, but so does anything that stops early. The decisive number was that **3,687 of 4,086 walks land exactly on the offset the header itself declares at `+0x30`**, next residual occurring 3 times. **Look for a value the format publishes that your reconstruction must reproduce**, and prefer it to any boundary of your own choosing — it cannot be tuned toward. This also resolved what `+0x30` pointed at, a question open since the format was first described: the answer fell out of a test aimed at something else.
- **A guard inside your own harness is a denominator you never see** (§26.10). The draw-range parser here carried an arbitrary cap rejecting group counts above 64 — a limit taken from character meshes, where nothing exceeds it. Vehicles are multi-part assets and legitimately have more, so the cap **silently discarded 286 of 372 files** and every vehicle statistic reported was computed on a 23% subset chosen by a made-up threshold. **The harness still printed a confident N/N.** That is the hardest variant of the denominator failure to spot, because nothing looks wrong: there is no warning, no residual, just a smaller N that nobody reads as small. **Make every filter count what it rejects and print it, even when you expect zero** — a report that does not mention exclusions looks identical whether there were none or hundreds. *(The clean team audited their own tooling for the same shape on hearing this, found an instance, and confirmed it had never fired — then said so explicitly as "skipped: 0" rather than leaving it implicit. That is the right follow-through.)*
  **Why this one was invisible rather than obvious:** the true maximum is **311** groups, so a cap of 64 was not a near-miss threshold, it was off by roughly **5×**. A slightly-too-tight limit trims a few outliers and the loss is noticeable; a wildly-too-tight one removes most of the population and leaves behind a **self-consistent survivor set** that parses cleanly, agrees with itself, and reports N/N. **The more wrong the threshold, the healthier the result looks.** Raising the cap here left all four conclusions intact and moved them from 86/86 to 372/372 — it widened the evidence rather than overturning it, which is the lucky outcome, not the guaranteed one.
- **Small-id fields reading 65542, 131072, 196609 are a packed word read whole** (§26.10). Vehicle draw-range "material ids" looked like nonsense until the pattern registered: every value is `(hi << 16) | lo` with a small `lo`. Masking recovered ids dense from zero in 86/86, and the high half turned out to be a sub-mesh index running 0,1,2,3. **A field that should hold small integers but holds values just above multiples of 65536 (or 256) is almost never corrupt — it is two fields.** Cheap to check, and it converts "this data is wrong" into a new field for free.
- **The same field can mean different things in different carriers of a shared structure** (§26.10). Sub-header `+0x0C` is exactly `1 + max(material id)` in `.ccmesh_pc` (241/241) and merely an **upper bound** in `.ccar_pc` (0/86 for equality, 86/86 for the bound) — a vehicle declares a material set larger than any one of its meshes uses. Shared machinery does not imply shared field semantics, and a claim measured on one carrier must be re-measured, not inherited, on the next. **Sibling of the convention-vs-rule lesson below, at field level rather than value level.**
- **A convention is not a rule until a second, unrelated carrier confirms it** (§26.10). `spec-vertex-format.md` §8.3 recorded "slot 0 = diffuse, slot 1 = normal" on the strength of 1,266/1,266 character-mesh entries — a real measurement, and still an over-generalisation. Vehicles falsified it immediately: the *same* sampler hash `0x2808EB90` sits at slot 1 in meshes and slot 0 in vehicles, so the index is only the entry's position and **the sampler is identified by the hash**. What looked like a format rule was "position 0 happened to be diffuse in every sample of the one carrier measured". **Before promoting a per-field meaning to a format rule, test it on a carrier that shares the mechanism but not the authoring conventions** — and when it breaks, fix it at the source rather than adding a per-format exception.
- **A property shared by every failure is not a cause unless the successes lack it — and sampling only failures can never show that** (§26.9, clean team). Chasing the vehicle walk bug they noticed every failing file read version 42, which looks like a discriminator until you check the passing set: **all 372 vehicles are version 42.** The comparison that matters is failures *against* successes, and a failure-only sample structurally cannot make it. Same family as the vacuous-control and dead-denominator lessons below: each is a missing negative case.
- **When handing over a fix, specify the invariant, not just the line to add** (§26.9). This team diagnosed the blob skip correctly and sent the formula; the receiving reader **already had that formula right**, and the defect was a redundant round-up one line *below* it. Transcribed literally, the handover scored **0/372**. The harness that found it was correct because of what it did *not* do — nothing rounds after the blob — and that absence was never stated. **Say what must not be there, not only what must.**
- **A bug that clusters by asset type is usually not asset-specific — it is the population that cannot hide it** (§26.9). A walk error presented as "vehicle-specific, 45% of files, rare elsewhere". It was a one-byte error in a blob skip shared by every carrier. Vehicles have arrays 1–4 *empty*, and the walk aligns only before an array it actually visits — so with nothing to re-align the cursor the error propagated intact to the landing site, while in meshes a populated array absorbed it and the identical bug was invisible. **Before accepting "format X is special", ask which structural property of X stops the error being masked** — that reframes a mystery as an expected consequence and usually points straight at the shared term.
- **When a claim of yours is challenged, test it against the challenger's oracle rather than re-running your own** (§26.9). Told that a 372/372 result could not be right, the useful move was to build *their* independent ground truth (version-9 plus matching check value, zero array arithmetic) and diff against it per file — 367/367 agreement, which simultaneously confirmed the result and proved the disagreement lay elsewhere. Re-running the original harness would have produced the same number and settled nothing. **A cross-check only counts if it can fail differently from the thing it is checking.**
- **An offset without an explicit base is not a location — write the derivation as code, and verify it against a magic** (§26.7, §26.9). This cost the implementation team real work **twice in one session**. First: "the material count is at outer sub-header `+0x0C`" — read against the wrong header, reads zero on every file. Second: "vehicles embed the structure at an offset given by their own header" — which field? They guessed, and got a 45% failure rate they reasonably attributed to the *walk* rather than to reaching it. **Both were correct sentences that were not implementable.** The fix that actually prevents a third occurrence is to give the derivation as executable lines, including the base, rather than as prose naming a field offset. And **always follow a computed offset with a check against a known magic**: it is free, and it converts a silent mislanding — which produces plausible garbage — into a clean, loud failure. Nearly every real bug this session was a silently-wrong offset.
- **Rule out the proposed cause even after you have found the real one** (§26.9). The suggested explanation for the vehicle failures was unconfirmed gate widths on arrays 5 and 6. Finding the true cause (sub-header location) did not settle that, so it was tested separately: the walk was run three ways — arrays 1–4, plus 5, plus 5 and 6 — giving **identical 372/372** results, and the gate fields read **zero in 384/384** files. Arrays 5 and 6 are never populated in shipped data at all. Had that been left as "probably not it", the next person would have spent a day on a dead end that is now closed with a number.
- **A match rate without an ambiguity count is not a measurement** (§26.7). The texture-binding search found a valid run in **3,954 / 3,954** material records — a perfect score, and nearly worthless, because it never asked *how many other positions in the same record would have satisfied the same test*. Stratifying by run length split that 100% into **1,285 records where exactly one position qualifies** (real) and **300 where six or more do** (the search picking arbitrarily, parameter hash `0x00000000`, selecting `missing.tga` and placeholder normals). The right question is never "did my rule find something" but **"how many things would my rule have found?"** — and the fix was two cheap guard conditions. Caught here only because the implementation team was about to wire it in; an unguarded reader would have bound placeholder textures to real surfaces and rendered something plausible. Sibling of the lane-granularity and denominator lessons: all three are failures to ask what the *negative* case looks like.
- **One exercised case proves one case, not the category — mutate and test at a finer grain than feels sufficient** (clean team, 2026-09-11). Their mutation suite caught a hole in their own *fixture*: a negative texture coordinate appeared only in the V lane, so a mutation dropping the sign on U specifically went uncaught. The reader was correct; the test merely proved signedness was handled *somewhere*, and a coarse "break the whole decode" mutation had passed and certified a half-blind fixture. **The same trap applies to every field with independent lanes or axes** — x/y/z of a position, the four blend-weight lanes, each texture-coordinate set, and (relevantly, for anything ahead) each channel or axis of an animation track. Applying it backwards to this team's own published claim found the evidence intact but **right by luck**: the signedness test qualified a channel if *either* lane carried a negative, and it happened that both do (U 31,513, V 22,186, and all three texture-coordinate sets). The spec now states it per lane. **Check whether your own passing test would still have passed had only one lane carried the property.**
- **"The verification ran" and "the verification was checked" are different claims** (§26.5). This project edits specs with hit-count-verified replace scripts precisely so a silent miss cannot happen. One did anyway: the script printed `MISS (0 hits)` and `all applied: False`, the output scrolled past unread, and a spec was relayed to the implementation team with a corrected section sitting directly beneath an uncorrected table row that still carried a confidence label on the wrong claim. **The safeguard worked perfectly and was simply not read.** Build the check so its failure is *impossible to proceed past* — or at minimum, read it before taking the next action, especially the action that publishes.
- **Ask whether a long-standing open item's own documentation already records the anomaly that breaks it** (§26.5, framing sharpened by the clean team). The `.rig_pc` rotation field sat OPEN across sessions while the paragraph describing it recorded 117 exceptions "all in vehicle rigs," and §7 separately noted a wing and an aircraft panel among them. Those were not curiosities to be explained later — they were the disconfirming cases, already written down, already labelled, needing only to be taken seriously. **Before opening a new investigation into an old open item, re-read what that item's own entry already says; anomalies get recorded honestly and then filed under HYPOTHESIS instead of being followed.** The cost here was two sessions and a wrong field label shipped to an implementation team.
- **A range check is not a type check** (§26.5). `spec-rig-format.md` called a field a rest rotation for two sessions on the strength of "every component within ±π in 22,157/22,274." The field is a parent-relative offset **in metres**, and offsets in metres fall inside ±3.14 for the same mundane reason angles in radians do — limbs are short. **The statistic never distinguished the two hypotheses; it only confirmed the numbers were small.** Before quoting a range as evidence of a field's type, ask which competing interpretations that range would also permit.
- **Exceptions that cluster by a physical property are a statement about units** (§26.5). The same row recorded that the 117 out-of-range bones were "all in vehicle rigs (`sp_vtol01`, `heli_*`)" and filed it as a curiosity with a HYPOTHESIS label about rotor bones exceeding one turn. It was the answer, written down and not followed: those are **large vehicles whose bones sit more than 3.14 metres apart**. When the outliers of a distribution sort perfectly by size, mass, or extent, the field is measuring that property.
- **A symmetry test can eliminate a whole hypothesis class without enumerating it** (§26.5). Rather than testing 12 Euler orders × 2 compositions × 6 axes one at a time, compare left/right mirrored bones: the field mirrors as `(−x, y, z)`, the **polar-vector** signature, in 5,958/6,760 pairs — identical to the position field's. Rotations are **pseudovectors** and mirror the other way round. One test killed every rotation convention at once, before any candidate had to be scored. **Look for a transformation the data must respect under a symmetry the asset already contains.**
- **Never fit two coordinate spaces by comparing bounding boxes** (§26.4). Matching a rig to its mesh, the rig's Z extent read 1.126 against the mesh's 0.404 — which looks exactly like a scale factor or an axis remap, and is neither. **It was one bone**: a parentless helper named `camera`, sitting a metre in front of the character. A bounding box is set by whatever is furthest out, and skeletal formats deliberately put helper, attachment and IK bones outside the body — so a rig's box is never the body's box, and the discrepancy will always present as a mysterious scale error. **Fit by correspondence instead**: the blend indices already say which bone owns which vertices, so a per-bone weighted centroid turns a vague shape match into a scoreable per-bone assertion, with a shuffled assignment as a free control (0.126 true vs 0.789 shuffled). Generalises past rigs: whenever two structures are linked by an explicit index, use the index, not the silhouette.
- **Near-ties across independent samples that disagree with each other are noise, not a result** (§26.4). Testing whether blend indices were offset from bone indices, two of six characters had some shift score marginally better than 0 — but they disagreed with each other on *both* the shift and the axis mapping. Picking the best-looking single number would have manufactured an indirection that does not exist. **When candidates are close, check whether the runners-up agree; disagreement among them is itself the measurement.**
- **When a population test throws a small anomaly, suspect the test's denominator before the format** (§26.3). Validating draw ranges, 22 of 1,831 meshes failed a "maxVertex < vertex count" check. It looked like a format irregularity worth writing up. It was not: those 22 are multi-channel meshes whose channels have *different* vertex counts, and the ranges are bounded by the largest channel, not by channel 0 which the test used.
