Bound correctly, 1,831/1,831. **Three separate findings in one session were nearly lost or mis-stated because of a wrong denominator** — a normaliser the hypothesis could move, a control the null passed for free, and this. On this kind of work the denominator is the characteristic failure mode, not an occasional slip; check it first, every time. **A fourth instance closed the loop: a published "428/428" for the material-count field was ~47% single-material meshes, where the expected value is 1 and any field that often reads 1 matches — caught not by this team but by the clean team, running the check back the other way.** Restricted to meshes where the test can fail it is 241/241 with the runner-up at 14.5%, so the finding held; but the honest figure is the restricted one. **That the discipline now works as a cross-check *between* teams rather than only as self-correction is a property of the process worth protecting.**
- **A ratio whose denominator moves with the hypothesis is not a measurement** (§26.1). Deciding signed vs unsigned texture coordinates, the first test measured how much a coordinate changes along a triangle edge *as a fraction of that reading's own coordinate range*. But the wrong reading inflates its own range ~64× by turning small negatives into huge positives — so it shrank its own denominator and "won" by 2.7×. An absolute delta reversed the verdict completely. **Before normalising anything, ask whether the hypothesis under test can move the normaliser.** Sibling of the vacuous-control and chance-rate lessons below; the same mistake wearing a third costume.
- **A screening test that the wrong answer passes trivially is worse than no test** (§26.1). The first pass scored candidate texture-coordinate encodings with "decodes to a half-float pair with |value| < 64" — which *denormals pass automatically*. It scored 0.94 on data that is not half-float at all, and that number went into a published spec as support for the wrong encoding. One look at the raw bytes (94.6% of values have a zero exponent field) settled it instantly. **Check what your screen does to the null hypothesis before quoting its hit rate.**
- **In decompiler output, a pointer index is in units of the pointed-to type** (§26). `spec-geometry-format.md` recorded a channel-record size byte at `+0x01` for two sessions. The source expression indexed an `int`-typed pointer by 1 — a **four**-byte advance. The real field is at `+0x04`, and with `+0x01` no size arithmetic replays at all. Whenever a decompiled expression mixes pointer types (`*(byte *)(int_ptr + n)`), convert the index to bytes before writing it into a spec. Cheap to check, and it silently poisons every downstream offset.
- **Falsify a proposed shortcut before building around it** (§26). The suggested route to the vertex layout was to find a D3D9 `CreateVertexDeclaration` call and read the layout out of it. One Ghidra query settled it: the binary makes **zero** COM vtable calls at that slot — or at `DrawIndexedPrimitive`, which is the real tell that the renderer is wrapped behind its own abstraction. Two minutes spent disproving the shortcut beat any amount of time spent making it work. Corollary: **a negative result at the right moment is cheap; the same negative result discovered late is expensive.**
  **⚠ CORRECTED 2026-09-13 — the “zero COM vtable calls” premise was itself wrong, and it cost the project a real trace for a full session.** The original scan searched for the instruction form `CALL [reg+disp]` and found 293 sites, zero at any D3D9 slot; the compiler actually emits a two-instruction idiom, `MOV reg,[reg+disp]` then `CALL reg`, which has 17,916 sites including `CreateVertexDeclaration` itself. **`CreateVertexDeclaration` IS called** (`FUN_00476ca0`), and reading what it does resolved the skinning-consumer question outright (`spec-rig-format.md` §11.1) — which is exactly the shortcut this lesson said not to build around. **The falsification instinct was right; the falsification itself was wrong, because the census pattern was too narrow to see the real calls.** Amended corollary: a shortcut-falsifying scan needs the same discipline as any other census — assert a known positive and confirm it fires before trusting a zero (see §28.9's methodology note).
- **A structure that declares its own length gives you a free exact test** (§26, and the same lesson Team B hit in `spec-texture-format.md` §4). The Mesh sub-block states the byte length of its `g`-file segment. That turns "does my understanding of the layout look plausible" into "does my replay consume exactly this many bytes and land on the expected sentinel" — a pass/fail on every shipped file, with no judgement involved. **Look for a declared-length or declared-count field before designing any statistical test; formats hand you oracles more often than you would expect.**
- **When a size formula nearly fits, the residual is the finding — never the tolerance** (Team B, `spec-texture-format.md` §4). That document carried a hedge about "the small rounding expected from block-compression's fixed minimum block size." It was not rounding. Mip chains stop at 4×4, and assuming they ran to 1×1 overshot by exactly the two missing levels — 16 bytes for BC1, 32 for BC3, every time. A constant, unexplained residual is a bug with a story attached, and writing a plausible-sounding tolerance around it hides the story for months.
- **A control over records where the predicate cannot fail dilutes itself** (§13, Team B's correction). The morph `+0` control read 1.3% — but 322 of its 324 "ascending" hits were single-element records, where "strictly ascending" has nothing to fail against. Over `N ≥ 2` the chance rate is 0.008%. The finding was already right; the *contrast* was understated ~160×. Sibling of the `+0x24` lesson above: before quoting a rate, restrict the denominator to trials that could have gone either way.
- **In a memory-image format, a stale pointer is a flag** (§17). Tree LOD presence entries are nonzero *heap pointers* dumped to disk; the loader only tests them for nonzero. Do not try to decode such values, and do not let their variation across files suggest structure — they vary because the build tool's heap did.
- **A format without a standalone parser is a runtime problem wearing a format's clothes** (§22). `.czn_pc` was picked precisely because it looked like a format pass — a big file set with an unexplained header. It has no parser to read: its structure exists only in the code that walks it section by section, inside the zone streaming subsystem. The tell, available early and cheaply, was that **no id value appears as an immediate anywhere in the binary** — when a loader never literal-checks its own header, the structure is data-driven and there is no single function to decompile.
- **Check whether your own tooling was wrong before concluding the data is** (§21). The `.czh_*` extension strings exist in the binary in the standard five-platform layout, which looked like proof that the inventory's "no extensions" for types 44/45 was a bug in this project's table parser. It was not: the registration function genuinely never references that table — a *different* subsystem owns it. The suspicion was reasonable and the check was cheap; skipping it would have published a correction that was itself wrong.
- **The existence of a file is not evidence of its contents** (§20). This project published, as HIGH CONFIDENCE, that `high_mips.vpp_pc`'s presence in the install corroborated the Low Mips reading. The archive is an empty 2 KB stub. The claim was ours, not a peer's, which is exactly why nobody re-examined it — an inherited inference deserves the same controls as a new one, and a confidence label records what was known when it was written, not what is known now.
- **Re-test "engine-wide" claims when a new subsystem opens** (§19). The rotate-6/XOR hash was labelled engine-wide on strong evidence and the docs went one step further — "a reimplementation needs exactly one". `.ctdg_pc` uses a *second* hash (table-driven CRC-32) for its registry key while using the rol6 one for speaker ids inside the same file. The breadth claim had hardened into an exclusivity claim nobody had tested; both specs are corrected.
- **A hash can be identified by its algebra, not its output** (§19). Proving the speaker ids are the rol6 hash needed no brute force: two ids from names differing in one character differ by exactly that character's XOR, which is that hash's signature and something CRC-32 provably cannot do. One structural identity beat a 3,917-candidate search that returned nothing.
- **A registration row gets you a constructor, not necessarily a parser** (§18). The `.csc_pc` constructor is 22 bytes and calls nothing — it stashes the buffer to a global and returns. The parser was found by enumerating that global's *readers*. Two formats now show this stash-only shape (morph types 11/12, csc 24), so when a registered ctor's body is tiny, stop reading it and start following its writes.
- **A negative result needs its chance rate as much as a positive one does** (§18). "0 / 31,898 keys are unit quaternions" is only informative because the same test fires on 30.2% of bytes known to be three floats. Without that control, a decisive refutation and a broken test look identical.
- **A rule that fits is not the loader's rule** (§17). The first tree replay found "align 32" placed the material records correctly on every file tried; the decompile showed the real rule (align 8, then `count × 8` pointer slots), which coincides on those files only because of their name-table lengths. When a replay rule is a guess, keep it labelled as one until the code confirms it — the coincidence breaks silently on the next file.
- **Independent reproduction by the other team is the best review this project gets** — Team B reproduced every `.anim_pc` statistic and found the one that didn't reconcile; their `.ccmesh_pc` extractor found the mandatory-pad rule that every derivation sample had happened to dodge. Hand them the populations, not just the specs.
- **A zero-information stratum can DEFLATE a real result, not only inflate a false one** (Team B, `.anim_pc` translation delta mode). Testing whether a per-key delta measures from the base or accumulates, the pooled figure was a weak 7.34× vs 4.83× — because 43.6% of the key-pairs sat in runs of length 1, where the two readings are **arithmetically identical** and no outcome can distinguish them. Stratified by run length the same data reads 1.8× at length 2–4 and 8.3× at length 5+. Every denominator lesson in this file up to now pointed one way — *a mixed population inflates a rate* — and here the mixed population was hiding a strong result behind a mediocre one. **The instinct trained on the inflation cases points away from the truth in this case, so drop the direction and keep the mechanism: ask whether part of your denominator is incapable of distinguishing the thing you are testing at all.** Sibling of the `+0` control lesson above (§13), which is this same mechanism seen from the other side.
- **A dose-response across a covariate beats a margin** (Team B, same test). What made that result convincing was not the ratio. It was that the two candidate readings are identical at run length 1, diverge mildly at 2–4, and sharply at 5+ — the exact signature of an error that compounds along a run — while the correct reading *improves* as runs lengthen, which is what it should do if longer runs are smoother real motion. **A coincidence can produce a large margin; it will not produce a monotone response to a covariate that the mechanism predicts the sign of in advance.** When a test has a natural ordering available (run length, frame count, element count, file size), score along it rather than pooling — the shape is a second, nearly free piece of evidence, and it is the piece a lucky fit cannot fake.
- **A correction must REPLACE the refuted claim's confidence label, not just add prose above it** (found 2026-09-12 by a label census across all 31 specs; fixed at `spec-geometry-format.md` §4.1). That bullet's prose was exemplary — a ⚠ marker, the original wording quoted, the refutation with its population (stride reads `2` in 17,934 of 17,934 shipped blocks, which no vertex buffer can be), and a pointer to the superseding document. But the **trailing confidence label was still the original one, arguing `HIGH CONFIDENCE — inferred` for the vertex-buffer reading the bullet had just refuted.** These documents are written to be skimmed by label, so the correction was invisible to exactly the reader most likely to rely on it. Worse, the stale label's qualifier asked for the wrong evidence ("not proven by cross-checking against an independently-known real vertex count") when what actually settled it was the stride field's own value. **Two generalisations. First, a correction inherits the frame of the error it fixes unless you deliberately re-examine the parts you did not rewrite** — the same failure as citing a binding-run figure for a draw-run claim. **Second, audit by label, not by prose:** a mechanical census of every labelled claim found in one pass what two prose readings of the same bullet had missed, because the defect was a *mismatch between* two parts that each read fine alone.
- **With N parallel investigators, "never subtract across populations you did not both define" stops being a discipline and becomes a structural constraint on the consolidator** (2026-09-12, six parallel sessions; the hazard was named by Team B before any of them reported). Every cross-boundary error in the preceding stretch was a cross-boundary *number* error — a binding-run figure cited for a draw-run claim, a "~69" that was a subtraction across two differently-defined populations, a 334/397-vs-328/518 near-collision — and each was hard to catch precisely because **each side's own usage was locally correct in isolation.** Six definers multiply that boundary by six, and the consolidator is the one person who sees all the numbers having made none of the measurements. **The cheap mitigation, now mandatory: every published figure carries what was counted, not just the count** — "3,687 of 3,691 clips-without-flag-`0x40` that walk cleanly", never "99.89%" and never a bare "3,687" — **and no two sessions' figures may be combined or subtracted without re-deriving.** A percentage is a number that has forgotten its population, which is exactly the information the boundary destroys.
- **A walk over empty blocks is a test that cannot fail** (2026-09-12, `.anim_pc`). A clip whose self-delimiting block walk landed perfectly looked like a clean confirmation; it was one of 12 tiny prop poses whose walk is **trivially satisfied by zeros** — every block empty, so every offset "correct" by construction. The audit this demands is cheap and should precede any walk statistic: **measure how much of each walk is empty, population-wide, and restrict the headline to files where the walk had something to get wrong.** Same shape as the single-element-record control (§13) and the 393/393 distinct-ids claim: the failing case has to be physically possible, and a structure made of zeros cannot disagree with any layout you propose.
- **A HEADING is a label too, and it goes stale the same way** (2026-09-12, found by Team B in their own document within minutes of reading the label-audit rule above, then reciprocally in two of ours). Theirs was a sub-heading still reading "… and it FAILS" hours after the subsection directly beneath it had retracted exactly that claim — they had fixed the top-level heading and written the retraction, and missed the sub-heading. Ours were `spec-morph-format.md` §7, titled "What is *not* resolved: the dequantisation" while §13 of the same file is titled "The dequantisation — RESOLVED" (with two `[OPEN.]` labels still standing on questions §13 answers outright), and `spec-anim-format.md` §6c.4, asserting "the residual is a **single population**" and "they are not two mechanisms, they are one", after the residual was shown to hold at least two kinds. **These documents are written to be skimmed by heading and label, so a stale heading is not cosmetic — it is the version most readers leave with.** The mechanism is the same one as the stale confidence label and the cross-team citation errors: **a mismatch between two parts that each read correctly in isolation**, which is why re-reading the prose never finds it. **The audit has to enumerate headings and labels independently of the text under them.** Cost of the check: one regex over every heading that contains an assertive word, then read each against its own section — 105 candidates across 31 documents, two real defects, about ten minutes.
- **When an audit and a conservative fix disagree, believe the fix** (2026-09-12). A cross-reference audit flagged 42 bare section references as mechanically fixable. The patch script, which re-checked each site for a nearby filename before rewriting it, declined about 30% of them and **refused outright to write one file where nothing matched** — all correctly: those references were already qualified through a form the audit's regex could not see (a range like "§4.1.1–§4.1.2", a comma list after one filename, a "new §X" phrasing). **The audit was the less careful instrument even though it was the one raising the alarm.** Two rules fall out: **gate every automated rewrite on a re-check at the point of the write, never on the survey that found the site** — the survey optimises for recall, the gate for precision, and you want the pessimist holding the pen. And **a fix script that refuses to run is reporting a result**, not failing; "nothing matched, refusing to write" is what correctly-already-correct looks like. Companion to the lesson that a verification reporting failure can itself be the bug — same asymmetry, opposite direction.
- **A mechanism's own name can encode which DIRECTION a call goes, and getting that backwards makes a real, fully-traceable mechanism look like a static-analysis dead end** (2026-09-12, `vint_callback_lua`). The class was named and described as “the C-function-exposed-to-Lua object type” — read naturally, a roster of engine functions Lua scripts can call. Traced end to end (all 7 static callers of its claim function, read as functions in their own right rather than assumed to be setup code), it does the opposite: it lets a **running Lua script register its own callback**, to be invoked **by the engine**, against a named target object. Under the first (wrong) reading, “the roster” looks unrecoverable by static means, because there is no fixed roster to find — which is exactly why the document's own prior text recommended a dynamic pass. Under the corrected reading, static tracing settles it completely, because the mechanism *is* the fixed thing and the registrations are the variable, unrecoverable part — which nobody needed anyway once the direction was right. **When a static approach stalls on a named mechanism, check whether the name's own implied direction is actually the one the code implements before reaching for a dynamic pass** — the stall can be the direction, not the method.
- **Disassembling more than one instance of a suspected trampoline family is what exposes the parametrised part of it — a single example cannot show it** (2026-09-12, the element-type registry). Reading one get/set trampoline pair looked like a fixed shape; reading all 6 (3 properties × get/set) showed the *only* thing that varies between them is which vtable slot they forward to, always exactly 2 slots apart — which is what actually proved the real structure (one accessor pair per named property, not per type). **A confidence label on a structural claim from a single disassembled instance should stay `HYPOTHESIS` until a second, independently-chosen instance is read and shown to vary only where the hypothesis predicts.**
- **A wide candidate search and a single deterministic formula are different instruments even when they test the same target, and only one of them is vulnerable to the ambiguity-1 vacuity trap** (2026-09-12, the final resolution of the entire `.anim_pc` `0x40` thread). Every earlier claim in this thread — the continuation search, the ambiguity count, the shift-control disagreement — tried up to 512 candidate block counts and asked whether *any* landed, which is exactly the wide-space vacuity this file has already documented twice. **The result that actually closed the thread used no search at all**: parse a count table, compute one specific size, walk once, check whether the cursor lands exactly on the target. That distinction is what let two shift controls (collapsing to 0.39% and 0.00%) and a cross-population misapplication control (collapsing a working 99.89% baseline to 0.03%, worse than chance) all behave as sharply as they did — a formula with one specific, correct answer fails hard when perturbed in any direction; a 512-wide search barely notices. **Before trusting a landing-on-target result, ask whether it came from a search over many candidates or a computation with one candidate** — the same oracle, the same target byte, and completely different evidential weight.
- **A day-long dispute between two teams' instruments can dissolve entirely once the actual mechanism is found, rather than resolve in either side's favour** (2026-09-12). This thread produced, in order: a refuted ambiguity claim, a refuted retraction of that refutation, a bounded 121/69-vs-135/55 split, and an unresolved instrument disagreement about which team's shift control was broken. **None of those questions turned out to have a right answer, because none of them were the real question** — every side was walking from the wrong starting cursor for the same reason, and once that was found, the entire stack of partial answers, controls, and counter-controls collapsed into one clean mechanism. **When a dispute keeps needing another control to settle the last one, consider that the disagreement itself may be a symptom of a missing structural piece, not of either instrument being wrong** — the fix was not a better control, it was reading one more function.
- **A decompiled call site with zero visible arguments is not evidence the callee takes none** (2026-09-12). Under no-analysis decompilation, register-passed arguments that the immediate caller only forwards — never itself reads — are invisible to the decompiler's pseudocode, even though the callee's own decompilation correctly recovers a full parameter list. The tell was a genuine four-parameter callee reached by an apparent zero-argument call. **When a call site looks information-free, check raw disassembly before concluding the function has no traceable inputs** — inverted from the usual direction of this caution: here the decompiler under-reported a real signal rather than over-reporting a fake one.
- **When a raw-binary string scan truncates a promising match, search for a shorter, distinctive substring instead of guessing the rest** (2026-09-12, the Havok source-path find). A raw Python byte-scan's context window cut off a promising string before its most useful part; re-searching for a *guessed* completion came up empty. Switching to a shorter, more distinctive fragment and letting Ghidra's defined-data string search return the whole string recovered it complete — a full Havok SDK source path, the single most decisive identification in this pass. **Two different string-search tools have different failure modes** (a fixed-width raw scan truncates; a defined-data search returns whatever the binary itself terminated the string at) — when one produces a partial result, switch tools rather than trying to patch the guess.
- **A clean split between “no middleware found” and “here is the Volition mechanism instead” is itself a complete, well-scoped result — do not treat the negative half as unfinished just because it has no positive content of its own** (2026-09-12, AI scoping). Six named middleware systems were checked and refuted with the same rigor the Havok identification used to *confirm* one, and the result is not weaker for being a negative — it is what makes “this is Volition's own code” a finding rather than a default assumption nobody checked.
- **A directory-walk can self-confirm by wandering into zero-padded reserved space, and the fix is to require it to land exactly on a size the format declares independently** (2026-09-12, the `_media.bnk_pc` wrapper). An early attempt at the chained record walk kept appearing to succeed on reserved, unused table space, because a record whose fields are all zero trivially satisfies `offset[i+1] = offset[i] + round_up(0, 0x800)` — the walk simply restates its own starting position forever and never raises. This is the same family as the walk-over-empty-blocks vacuity already in this file, one layer down: there, an empty *block* couldn't fail; here, an all-zero *record* can't either. **The general fix, not specific to either case: require a walk to terminate exactly on an independently-declared size (here, the outer container's own recorded entry size), never merely on “it didn't raise”** — 89,631 records across 536 files were only trustworthy once that check was added.
- **"Re-measure it directly" only beats "infer it" when the direct measurement uses the AUTHORITATIVE implementation** (Team B, 2026-09-12, reported by them as their own error). They had estimated the third `.anim_pc` sub-population at ~69 by subtracting one team's failure count from another's, **correctly rejected that as "subtracting across populations we didn't both define"**, and re-measured directly to get 55. A later census against the **actual shipping reader** returned 69: the subtraction had been right, and the direct measurement wrong, because their probe was asking its own *private reimplementation* of the walk logic — which disagrees with the shipping reader on 14 clips. **They had swapped an unvalidated inference for an unvalidated measurement and felt more rigorous for it.** The rule that survives is not "trust inference over measurement"; it is that **a measurement inherits the authority of the implementation that produced it, and a second implementation of a rule is a hypothesis about that rule, not an oracle for it.** Two corollaries they drew and both are worth keeping. **Deliberate, documented divergence is a tool** — their vehicle validator keeps a pre-tightening copy of the reader *on purpose*, to measure what the current one filters out. **Undocumented divergence assumed identical is a bug with two places to hide**, and you cannot tell which one is wrong. So when a harness reimplements shipping logic, have it **print a disagreement count against the real reader on every run, with the expected value stated inline as 0** — cheap, permanent, and it converts a silent drift into a failing line.
- **Stratification prevents wrong confirmations as often as it rescues right ones** (2026-09-12, `.anim_pc` inter-record blending). Pooled and unstratified, the blend test would have read **9.89× vs 7.34× in favour of blending — a clean, publishable confirmation of the wrong answer.** Stratified by span it inverts: spans 2–4 favour blend decisively, spans 5+ favour no-blend decisively, with a genuine monotone crossover near span 4, so the honest verdict is **OPEN**, not resolved either way. Note the direction, because it completes the pair: the earlier lesson had stratification *recovering* a real result that a zero-information stratum was diluting; here it *blocks* a false one that pooling would have manufactured. **Same instrument, opposite errors, which is why it belongs in the default procedure rather than in the toolbox for suspicious results.** Two checks are what make a crossover believable rather than noise, and both are cheap: a **model-divergence check** (confirm the two strata genuinely predict different things, so the crossover is not an artefact of the metric going flat) and a **base-difference control** (here, ruling out "long spans are just static"). Also worth copying: their directional lean was stated as *weak* and argued from mechanism — a real blend-toward-the-next-record decoder should get **better** as its prediction weight approaches 1 at long spans, not 3× worse — rather than from the margin.
- **An overloaded noun has now caused three separate real errors in this project; treat it as a known defect class, not bad luck.** (1) `spec-anim-format.md` §6c.2's prose said "sample bytes" where the disassembly meant "control record bytes" — that single collision made the partner team's replication of a **correct** finding fail. (2) "run" meant both *binding run* and *draw run*, and a binding-run figure cited for a draw-run claim published a relation **backwards** (draw ranges exceed the material count in 392 of 393 files). (3) "lands" meant both *lands on the declared `+0x30` endpoint* and *lands when the walk continues past it* — under which **0 of 518** bit-carrying clips land in the first sense while **328 of 518** land in the second, so "clips that land successfully also carry the bit" is true in one sense and false in the other. The pattern: **a term with two referents survives inside one document because each local use is correct, then produces a wrong claim the first time it crosses a section or team boundary.** The fix is mechanical and costs a word: **never write the bare noun — write which kind, every single time**, in prose, in table headers, in harness variable names and in figure captions. If a document needs both senses, name them differently and say so once at the top.
- **Before weakening a claim whose evidence looks thin, check whether the CAPTURE was already complete** (2026-09-12, morph §14). I reviewed a raw-assembly window, found its register dataflow did not close, and proposed the careful-looking remedy: label the block as filtered and drop the confidence on the one constant that depended on the omitted instructions. **The right fix was the opposite.** The session went back to its own tool output and found the **entire address-contiguous block had been captured all along** — the filtering was self-inflicted in the write-up, not a limit of the evidence — so the missing instruction was recovered and the constant went **up** to `CONFIRMED — disassembly`. The diagnosis was right and the remedy was wrong, which is a specific and repeatable trap: **a reviewer sees the artefact, not the capture, and so reaches for the only lever visible from there, which is the label.** Ask "is the evidence incomplete, or is the *write-up* incomplete?" first — they need opposite responses, and only one of them costs you a finding.
- **An ESP-relative displacement that seems to change mid-function is often the same slot after a frame adjustment** (2026-09-12). A `SUB ESP,0x10` shifts every subsequent `[ESP+n]` by sixteen, so `[ESP+0x68/0x6c/0x70]` after it address exactly the same three floats as `[ESP+0x58/0x5c/0x60]` before it. Read as two different buffers, that looks like the code writing one place and reading another — which is precisely the shape of evidence that would *refute* an in-place-modification claim, so the misreading is not harmless. **Track the frame pointer's own arithmetic before concluding that two displacements are two locations**, the same way a pointer index has to be converted to bytes before it goes in a spec: in both cases the decompiled or disassembled text is correct and the naive reading of it is not.
- **"Exactly one candidate fits" is only informative when the candidate space is narrow — the ambiguity-count safeguard has its own vacuity mode** (2026-09-12, `.anim_pc`). An exhaustive search for a payload length against a fixed target returned **ambiguity exactly 1 wherever it found a fit**, which reads like decisive evidence that the fit is a real location. A shift control killed it: **17–22 of 55 clips produced a fit regardless of the starting offset** — the space was wide enough that a unique fit carries no information at all. The safeguard is sound and this project has relied on it correctly elsewhere; what it needs is its **denominator**, exactly like a rate. **Publish the size of the candidate space alongside any ambiguity count, and control it by shifting the anchor.** If a unique fit appears at an offset where no structure can exist, uniqueness was never the evidence. **Confirmed the hard way the same day:** Team B ran exactly that control on the `.anim_pc` continuation search over a **512-wide** candidate space — **333 of 518 clips found a working count from a deliberately wrong start against 328 of 518 from the real one, both at ambiguity 1.00.** The wrong start did *better*. A headline claim that the continuation was "a location, not a coincidental fit" was withdrawn on that basis.
- **Matching your own written spec is not matching the code that ships** (2026-09-12, the sharpest result of the day). **Two independently written reimplementations of the same correct spec both diverged from the shipping reader, and by different amounts** — the partner team's by 14 clips, ours by 6 — producing three mutually inconsistent splits of one 518-clip population, none of which was arithmetically wrong. The spec was not at fault; each reimplementation had silently resolved something the spec left implicit, and *differently*. **A spec is a hypothesis about the code until something replays against the code itself**, so a figure's authority comes from the implementation that produced it and must be stated with it: say "by the shipping reader" or "by our walker", never just the number. Corollary for how we work with the other team: when two teams' figures disagree, the question is not which number is right but **which implementation each came from** — that resolves it far faster than re-measuring, and twice today it resolved it completely. **⚠ But qualified the same day, and the qualification matters as much as the rule: naming the implementation is not the same as establishing its authority — see the next lesson.**
- **After correcting a number, grep the file for the old value** (Team B's rule, 2026-09-12, and cheaper than either audit that found the defect). Their own document carried superseded 55/135 figures in one section hours after another section had replaced them with 69/121 — the same stale-claim class as the stale heading and the stale confidence label, **found twice in one file in one day, both times by an audit aimed at something else, and neither findable by re-reading the prose, because the prose was correct both times.** Five seconds, no reliance on remembering every place you wrote it. Do it for the old value *and* for any percentage derived from it.
- **A wrong interpretation of a correctly-decoded number is a different defect class from a wrong decode, and conflating them makes a sound decode look unsafe** (Team B, 2026-09-12, on the rigid-bone null). Their hypothesis — a bone is rigid, so a translation track's magnitude should stay near-constant — failed badly: real spread median 1.2687 against an axis-shuffled control at 1.0572, the real reading **looser** than a control that destroys vector coherence, winning only 32.3% of tracks. The physical claim was not wrong; it was **misapplied to a population selected for the opposite property**. **150,466 of 166,297 tracks (90.5%) carry zero translation keys** — translation is stored only for bones that actually translate, so of course magnitude varies among the ones that have entries. The population accounting was the finding; the null was the route to it. And the decode arithmetic (`base/64 + delta/4000`) was never in question and stands independently confirmed. **Say which layer failed — the number, or the story about the number.**
- **A generic container or copy utility can look domain-specific from its call site alone; read the callee before trusting the analogy** (2026-09-12, vehicle materials). A function reached from a material walk was about to be written up as "material-name resolution", by analogy with a genuinely domain-specific lookup read minutes earlier in the same chain. Its body refuted that immediately: TLS allocation and generic 16-byte-element pointer fixups, a chunked-list utility with no material or name semantics anywhere in it. **The call site tells you what the caller wanted, not what the callee is** — and in a large engine the same fixup helper serves dozens of unrelated subsystems, so an analogy drawn from a neighbouring call is exactly the kind that feels strongest and is worth least.
- **When a structure declares its own byte length, replay which byte position that length is measured FROM** (2026-09-12, vehicle materials). Measuring consumed bytes from "just after the size field" instead of "the size field's own position" undercounted by 4 on every record — so the first material parsed perfectly and **every one after it looked corrupt**, while the parser was structurally correct throughout. That signature is worth memorising: **parses cleanly once, then degrades** means a per-record offset error, not a layout misunderstanding. Related to the declared-length lesson above — a self-declaring structure hands you a free exact oracle, but only once you know the origin its declaration is relative to, and that origin is a fact to read off the disassembly rather than assume.
- **Check that the thing exists before mounting a hunt for it — a registration slot can be a literal zero** (2026-09-12, `.czn_pc`). The plan was the technique this project had already validated repeatedly: the constructor is vtable-dispatched, so it has no xrefs and Ghidra defines no function there, so find it by data-side discovery. **That precondition was false here.** Both zone rows in the master resource table carry a **literal `0` in the constructor *and* destructor fields**, and the dispatcher has an explicit null-constructor branch that marks the resource loaded without running any type-specific code. There was never a function to find. **Two field reads distinguish "hidden behind indirection" from "absent", and the cost of not doing them is an unbounded search for a nonexistent target** — which is the worst-shaped task there is, because no amount of not-finding it is ever evidence. The trap is specifically that the technique was *correct for every previous format*: **a method validated on its last five targets is exactly the one you stop checking the preconditions of.**
- **An unexplained absence is an open question; a mechanically explained absence closes it** (2026-09-12, `.czn_pc`). "No zone id is literal-checked anywhere in the binary" sat in this file for sessions as an anomaly — unsettling, unexplained, and quietly implying that a cleverer search might still find the check. Tracing a confirmed zone-content string into the executable found the reason: a **generic named-property mechanism** — lowercase-string hash, a 40-bucket name→ordinal resolver, a property-bag class with bulk-stream-load — so ids are resolved **by name at runtime** and there is nothing for a literal search to find, ever. **The finding did not answer the original question; it dissolved it**, which is a strictly better outcome than another negative result, and it converts "keep looking" into "stop looking, look here instead". When an absence keeps recurring across passes, **stop testing for the missing thing and go find the mechanism that would make it missing.**
- **Set-verify a shortfall; do not count-match it** (2026-09-12, `.czn_pc`). An independent reproduction recovered validated Mesh blocks from **1,002 of 1,083 pairs (92.5%)**, and the 81-file shortfall matched the count of an already-known population (the zero-byte `.gzn_pc` files). **Matching counts is weak evidence that would have passed just as happily if the two 81-file sets were disjoint**; they checked **set equality** instead and it held exactly. The stronger check costs one set comparison and it is the difference between "the shortfall is consistent with the known cause" and "the shortfall **is** the known cause". Whenever a residual happens to equal the size of something you already understand, that coincidence is the moment to compare membership — it is also precisely when the weaker check feels most convincing.
- **A diligence measure performed as habit stops being a check** (Team B, 2026-09-12, and they were explicit that the obvious reading of their error is the wrong one). The obvious reading is "they forgot a control". What actually happened is worse and much easier to repeat: **they computed and published an ambiguity count precisely *because* this project's discipline demands one**, offered it as a rigor-driven headline finding — and never asked of that number the one question the same discipline asks of every other number, *what would a failing case look like*. The safeguard was performed, not applied. **Every number offered as evidence needs its own falsifying control, and the numbers most likely to escape that requirement are the ones produced specifically in order to look rigorous** — because they arrive already wearing the costume of a check, so they read as the thing that validates the other numbers rather than as another claim needing validation. Ask it of controls, of ambiguity counts, of chance rates, of population censuses: *how would this have come out if the underlying thing were not true?*
- **Before deferring to an authority, ask what population validated it — a validation that never exercised the behaviour in dispute confers no authority over it** (Team B, 2026-09-12, and they offered it specifically for this file). The shipping reader had become the tie-breaker between three disagreeing implementations, on the strength of being confirmed against **3,687 clips that land cleanly**. Then the disputed construct turned out to be an 8-byte control record with a **zero span field** — and **not one of those 3,687 clips contains one.** All 25 such records in the corpus sit in the 14 clips actually under dispute. So the reader's validated population is precisely the population that cannot speak to the question. **“This is what ships” had quietly become “this is correct”**, and I repeated the substitution when advising a parallel session to prefer the shipping reader. Keep the earlier rule — a figure must name the implementation that produced it — and add this: **the implementation's authority is scoped to the behaviours its validation actually exercised**, so for a disputed construct, check whether any validated case contains it before letting the authority settle anything. The generalisation past readers: an oracle, a reference implementation, a golden dataset and a spec all have validated domains, and the interesting cases are the ones that fall outside them — which is exactly where they get cited most confidently.
- **“Cause established” is not “correctness established”, and the gap is where a good result becomes an overstated one** (Team B, 2026-09-12). They byte-verified *why* two readers disagreed — unanimous 14 of 14, rival causes at 0 of 14, both readers entering at the identical cursor — which is about as clean as a causal finding gets. Then they caught themselves treating it as settling *which reader was right*, which it does not: knowing the divergence is one `span == 0` check tells you nothing about whether skipping such a record is correct behaviour or a symptom of having already drifted off the rails. **A perfectly clean causal explanation creates a strong pull toward the conclusion that the thing is now resolved** — and the tell that it is not is that the available oracle is one-sided: theirs could only ever *confirm* the skip, never refute it, since a miss can have any cause. **When an oracle can only fire in one direction, the most it can give you is a bound.** They recorded the bound (121/69 versus 135/55, with the condition that decides it) and marked both of their own earlier published figures as overstated, which is the right shape for the answer.
- **When one instrument underpins several findings, its failure is CORRELATED, not distributed — so audit the list, not the conclusions one at a time** (Team B's rule, 2026-09-12, and the most immediately actionable thing in this file). Discrediting the `.anim_pc` continuation search did not invalidate its conclusions one by one as each was re-examined; **they were all already wrong together, and had been since the instrument was built.** Their “extra = 39 blocks in 310 clips” went the same way as the ambiguity claim, and for the same reason — a shifted control reproduced the identical tight clustering centred on 42, the number displaced by exactly the control offset. **Three of their `0x40` claims fell; the two that survived are precisely the two that never touched the search.** So the cheapest audit after an instrument is discredited is not re-testing each finding: it is **enumerating everything that read off that instrument and marking the whole list suspect at once**, then checking which items have an independent route to the same conclusion. Corollary worth keeping: **a finding that survives its instrument's discrediting was never really resting on it** — which is a useful thing to learn about your own evidence, and the turn-in-place signature surviving here (its measurement never involved the walk) is the example to point at.
- **When two implementations of the same predicate disagree, check which one DISCRIMINATES under its own control before diffing code** (2026-09-12). Two shift controls on the identical anchor/hit design agreed closely at the real start (334 vs 328 of 518) and diverged catastrophically under a shift (7.92% vs 64.29%) — one dropped 8× from its real-start rate, the other stayed flat or rose. **A control that shows no separation between the true anchor and a wrong one is not measuring anything, regardless of which implementation is “correct” in some other sense** — so the non-discriminating implementation is the one to suspect first, and the specific mechanism to check is whichever one's per-step validation is looser (a walker that rarely desyncs on garbage input will find *something* in a wide candidate search almost anywhere, by pure combinatorics). **This is a cheap, information-rich comparison to run before any line-by-line diff**: it does not tell you which implementation matches the real format, but it tells you which one's *control* is informative, and that is where a diff should start looking. **Vindicated the same day, and by exactly the mechanism predicted**: the desync-rate diagnostic this lesson recommends in place of a cold diff — real 5.92%, wrong-anchor 6.27% (indistinguishable), correct-anchor 2.04% (a third of real) — was the specific measurement that confirmed the anchor bug, and it took one number, not a line-by-line read of unfamiliar code.
- **Reproducing a real-start figure across two independent implementations is not the same as reproducing the finding** (2026-09-12, same event). The two implementations agree well on the real-start count and disagree entirely on the shifted one — so “both sides measured 328ish real hits” is true and nearly useless on its own, because the entire evidential weight of this kind of claim lives in the *contrast* with the control, not in the headline number. **Report agreement or disagreement per row of a control table, never as one pooled verdict** — two instruments can agree on the easy number and disagree entirely on the number that was supposed to validate it.
- **“Shift it by N bytes” does not say WHICH anchor, and the wrong choice can corrupt the part of the walk a control is supposed to leave intact** (2026-09-12, the actual resolution of the anchor dispute above, after five relays of back-and-forth). Team B's shift control applied `+7` to the payload **start**, which also perturbs the declared-track-count walk — the part meant to remain a fixed, verified reference point so that only the thing under test (the continuation) is disturbed. Ours applied the same `+7` to `end_of_declared_walk` instead, leaving the declared walk untouched and perturbing only the continuation. **Both sides described their control the same way in prose (“shift by 7”) and had genuinely different instruments.** The tell was exactly what §5 already prescribes — check which instrument discriminates before diffing code — but the *cause*, once found, was not a coding bug in the ordinary sense: it was an **under-specified control design** that had two valid-sounding readings, and each team picked one without knowing there was a choice. **When describing a shift, name the anchor explicitly and say what stays fixed** — “shift the continuation's start cursor by N, holding the declared walk's own result constant” is a control; “shift by N bytes” is an ambiguity waiting to be two different experiments.
- **A refutation is scoped to the model it tested, not to every claim that mentions the same field** (2026-09-12, `.anim_pc` header `+0x06`). This project refuted “`+0x06` × tracks × bytes-per-key” at 0.55 bytes per frame-track, and that refutation stands. It was *not* a refutation of `+0x06` being a duration or frame count — it killed the **payload being a uniform frame-indexed array**, which is a different claim that happened to contain the same field. `+0x06` is now identified as **total clip duration**, and the reconciliation is that keys are sparse and each holds for a variable number of frames, so a naive frames×tracks array over-counts by roughly the mean gap between keys (median 2.214) — **the same arithmetic that refuted the old model predicts the new one.** The practical rule: when recording a refutation, write down **which conjunction failed**, because a refuted compound claim quietly poisons every component it mentioned, and that residue is what stops a field from being re-examined for sessions. Here it stalled an identification that two independent measurements then made straightforwardly.
- **A plumbing bug and a genuine all-zero field produce the same output; only an independent route tells them apart** (2026-09-12, vertex fields — and the sharpest version of the “0 of N means suspect plumbing” rule this project has hit). In a single afternoon one session produced **two real all-zero findings and two fake ones**. The fakes: a wrong length-field **measurement origin**, and an offset assumed absolute when it was **blob-relative**. Both yielded uniform zeros that read exactly like format facts. The real ones — channel record `+0x08` and component group B, each zero in 26,601 of 26,601 records across all six carriers — are only distinguishable from the fakes **because the disassembly independently says neither channel-array walker touches `+0x08` at all.** Population size does not help: 26,601 of 26,601 is exactly what a wrong base address also gives you, and a bigger population makes the fake *more* convincing, not less. **So never publish “this field is always zero” on file-side evidence alone — pair it with the code not reading the field, or with a second independently-derived route to the same bytes.** Note also that component group B is zero in live, arithmetically-used code, so “always zero” does not imply “dead”, and the two must not be conflated when writing it up.
- **A control has a population and a predicate of its own, and “we both ran the shift control” is not agreement** (2026-09-12). Two teams ran the same-named control on the same phenomenon and got **64.3% versus 9.3%** — a sevenfold disagreement in the *control*, while the real measurements agreed closely. The cause was almost certainly not the data and not the denominator: one side's predicate searched **512 candidate block counts** and asked whether any landed, the other **continued a single walk** and asked whether it landed. A wide predicate saturates and shows no separation under shift; a narrow one separates strongly. **So a control inherits the vacuity of the predicate it wraps** — shifting the anchor of a search that would succeed anywhere tells you nothing, and the result looks like a refutation. **State the candidate-space width of the control, not just of the measurement**, and when two controls disagree, compare predicates before comparing populations.
- **Check whether a proposed reconciliation can arithmetically account for the gap before accepting it** (2026-09-12). Faced with the disagreement above, the natural and plausible-sounding explanation was denominator dilution — their population includes 121 clips that fail the walk outright and ours does not. **A one-line feasibility bound killed it:** even granting that *every* one of those 121 hit spuriously under shift, the ceiling is **30.5%** against an observed **64.3%**, short by a factor of two. This is the range-check-before-fit rule pointed at an *explanation* rather than at a formula, and it is just as cheap there. **An explanation that is qualitatively right in direction can still be quantitatively impossible, and “the direction is right” is the most persuasive form a wrong answer takes** — it is also how a wrong reconciliation ends a thread that should have stayed open.
- **Read the instrument's own stated design before theorising about why two runs of it disagree** (2026-09-12, mine, and the second instance of this failure in one day). Two shift controls on the same phenomenon disagreed sevenfold, and I proposed that they used predicates of different candidate-space width — a tidy explanation that made both results correct. **Our own spec section stated, in the sentence introducing the measurement, that it used the same 512-candidate design and the same displacement.** I had read that section minutes earlier to verify the numbers in it. The tidy explanation was available and the disconfirming sentence was in the document, and I reached for the explanation — the same shape as attributing domain semantics to a generic utility from its call site, which is a lesson three bullets up, written the same afternoon. **Worse, the wrong explanation was comforting**: it made both teams right and closed the thread, where the truth is that the two instruments are provably incompatible (their control's numerator exceeds the ceiling our own numerator allows) and somebody's control does not do what it says. **A reconciliation that leaves everyone correct deserves more suspicion than one that does not, and the first place to look is always the design statement, not the results.** **Validated a second time within the hour**: Team B's own candidate explanation for the same disagreement (a weaker hit-predicate on one side) was tested directly and refuted by sign before it could stand for even an hour, and they flagged it as the identical shape of mistake as mine — just caught at a different speed. Mine was caught by re-reading a design document already in hand; theirs by taking one direct measurement before publishing. **Both routes work; the second is faster and should be tried first when it is cheap.**
- **A weaker predicate can only produce an equal-or-higher rate under a shift control, never a lower one — so a shift result below a known weak-predicate baseline refutes “weak predicate” by sign, no further argument needed** (Team B, 2026-09-12). Proposed to explain a sevenfold control disagreement, and tested directly: their weak predicate scored **75.29%** under shift, their strict one **64.29%**, and the disputed result was **9.32%** — below both. A weaker acceptance criterion is a strict superset of a stricter one's hits, so it cannot ever score lower under the same shift; **the direction of the gap, not its size, is what settles it.** When two explanations for a discrepancy make opposite-sign predictions, check the sign before running anything more elaborate — it is often decisive on data you already have.
- **A pattern noticed from a handful of hand-picked examples needs the full population checked before it is written up, or the exception gets silently dropped** (Team B, 2026-09-12). A residue pattern (“multiples of one half”) looked clean across six examples chosen while writing it up; checking the full set of 20 found one exception (`brute_grap_entr_a`, `71/15 = 4.733`) that the six-example version had simply never touched. **This is not the standing “never generalise from one small sample” rule (§5) restated — it is sharper: the sample was not disclosed as partial, and the exception was not deliberately set aside, it was dropped by the act of picking examples for illustration rather than checking exhaustively.** Corrected in place rather than silently, per this project's own standing discipline. Whenever a pattern is being written up from “examples” rather than from a stated population, that is the moment to run it against the full set before the prose hardens around the clean version.
- **The same trap recurred in two different channels within one day, and naming it once makes both instances the same lesson rather than two** (Team B, 2026-09-12): a property confirmed on one carrier or one channel does not automatically survive to another. `.anim_pc` translation tracks are 90.5% empty (150,466 of 166,297) — rigidity failed as a test not because the physical claim was wrong but because it was applied to a population selected for the opposite property. Tangent handedness is `CONFIRMED` on five carriers and fails on vehicles at 17.94% (independently reproduced at 17.9837%) — the same shape, one channel over, a spatial encoding instead of a temporal one. **Neither failure was a measurement error; both were a scope that had quietly widened past what was actually tested.** The generalisable check, cheap enough to run by default: **before trusting a `CONFIRMED` label across a new carrier, channel, or population, ask what population the confirmation actually ran over** — the same question that resolved the shipping-reader authority dispute two lessons up, now recurring as a property check rather than an implementation check. Same question, asked of a different kind of thing, catches both.
- **A true fact about your own instrument does not transfer to a claim about a different instrument, even when both produce the same symptom** (2026-09-12, the terrain/zone-data reconciliation). Asked to explain why Team B's validator reported 0% inline-mode blocks, the available, correct-sounding explanation was that **our own** validator (`mesh_scan.py`) is structurally blind to inline blocks by construction — true, and offered as “the most likely explanation” for their matching symptom. It does not hold: Team B's own code explicitly attempts inline parsing, so their validator has different properties and their 0% needs its own explanation, not a borrowed one. **Matching output (“0% either way”) is not evidence of a matching cause when the two instruments producing it are not the same instrument.** The fix that would have caught this before publishing: ask what the OTHER team's code actually does before offering an explanation for their result, not just what yours does — the same discipline as checking an instrument's own stated design before theorising about a disagreement (§5, earlier today), one level further out: here the disagreement was between two SYMPTOMS, and the trap was assuming a shared cause without checking the second instrument at all. **Labelling the explanation `HYPOTHESIS — unconfirmed` rather than asserting it, which this pass did, is what kept the refutation cheap** — one paragraph corrected in place, not a re-investigation of a claim that had hardened into fact.
- **An apparent CONTRADICTION between two numbers needs the same "are these actually the same measurement" scrutiny as an apparent agreement — the habit only existed for one direction until Team B generalised it after the lesson above** (2026-09-12). Team B's own 0/14,920 stood as a clean, well-controlled negative; the mistake was concluding it *contradicted* this project's ~23% inline-mode figure without first checking whether that figure came from a comparable full-validation test — it didn't (§7.3's own bookend check is tied to g-file cross-referencing and was never run as a from-scratch full replay on inline content), so the two numbers may not be measuring the same thing at all, and there may be no contradiction to resolve. **This is the same instrument-comparison discipline as the lesson above, applied to disagreement instead of agreement**: two teams had already learned to ask "is this the same measurement" when two figures matched suspiciously well (the real-start-reproduction lesson, and the shift-control-agreement lesson before it); this is the missing other half — ask the identical question when two figures *disagree*, before spending effort explaining a gap that might not exist. **Check "same measurement?" first, regardless of whether the two numbers agree or fight — the direction of the mismatch is not what tells you whether the check is needed.**
- **Vertex semantics resolved entirely on the GPU, never the CPU, is a real architectural fact discovered while chasing a much narrower question, and it explains a whole class of prior stalls, not just this one** (2026-09-12, tree layout codes). Tracing the tree loader chain end to end found that no CPU-side code reads individual vertex-field bytes for *any* layout code — the loader only computes strides and pointers, and semantic interpretation happens in shader code this project has not opened. **This retroactively explains why the CPU-side disassembly chain has repeatedly gone quiet exactly at the point where a field's *meaning* rather than its *size* was in question** (the normal's 4th byte, component group B, and now trees' position field). It does not mean these fields are unrecoverable — the normal and tangent for codes 11/13 were recovered from the file side, by the same unit-vector test used everywhere else — but it means a CPU disassembly chain going silent on a semantic question is not evidence of dead-end tooling, it is evidence of looking on the wrong processor.
- **Checking a calling convention against raw disassembly rather than trusting the decompiler's own variable names is what catches both a genuinely new field and a fabricated return value, in the same pass** (2026-09-12, vehicle materials). This component's worst published error was exactly a plausible-looking decompiler substitution taken at face value. Reapplying that specific caution here — confirmed the existing table's two arguments were right, but also surfaced a previously undocumented header field the decompiler's own naming had given no reason to suspect, **and** caught that a function's apparent "return value" was an unassigned register, not a computed result. **A caution earned from one bad label is worth re-running even after the labels it checks turn out fine** — the check paid for itself twice on data that would have looked correct without it.
- **A census of an instruction pattern is only as good as the exact form the compiler emits, and it silently produced a 60× undercount that read as a clean, confirming negative** (2026-09-13, the skinning-consumer trace). Scanning for `CALL [reg+disp]` found 293 sites across 3.5M instructions and zero at any D3D9 device slot — confirming, apparently, a standing project claim that the binary makes no such calls. The compiler does not emit that instruction form here; it emits `MOV reg2,[reg1+disp]` then `CALL reg2`, and counting that two-instruction idiom instead found **17,916** virtual-call sites, `CreateVertexDeclaration` among them. **The scan was caught only because it was made to assert a known positive** — a function already read in decompilation and known to make two vtable calls — **and that assertion failed, printing a plain `false` rather than a silent pass.** Without that check the zero would have been published as confirmation of an existing claim, compounding rather than catching the error. This is two lessons already in this file (state the predicate, not the conclusion; put a known-positive in every scan and assert it fires) landing on the same event, and it is the **second** time in this project a uniform zero has turned out to be a connection failure rather than a data fact. **A vtable-call census needs its known-positive assertion at least as much as any other scan does — arguably more, since "the binary never calls this" is exactly the kind of negative that gets cited by later work as settled ground, and this one was cited three times before it was checked.**
- **Refusing to publish a number is sometimes the correct result of building the harness, not a failure to finish it** (2026-09-13, same trace). A synthetic-pose harness produced a clean, headline-shaped figure — a 3.4× median and 205× p99 for cross-limb edges — that looked ready to publish. **Its first version had no same-limb population to compare against at all.** Adding the missing comparison group showed same-limb edges sitting at p90 3.8× too — the synthetic pose itself was scrambling the whole mesh, not selectively stressing cross-limb geometry, so the headline number was measuring the test harness's own pose generator, not the seam phenomenon it was built to isolate. **The missing control group was the error, not a step skipped on the way to a number** — a result with only the population you expected to see something in is not yet a result, it is half of one.
- **When a claim bundles an anchor assumption together with a walk rule, test them as two separate predicates or a wrong anchor will masquerade as noise in the walk** (Team B, 2026-09-13, the audio `_media.bnk_pc` corrections). "Record 0's offset is always `0x800`" and the chain-walk rule were originally verified together, so the 258 of 536 files where record 0 sits somewhere else looked like ordinary chain variance rather than what they actually were — a wrong anchor formula. Deliberately separating "where does record 0 sit" from "how does the chain advance from there" as two independently-tested claims caught it: the real anchor is `round_up(0x20 + recordCount*16, 0x800)`, and `0x800` is simply what that formula gives whenever the directory is small enough — the common case, not the rule. **Two claims that are usually true together are not thereby one claim** — test the anchor and the walk separately even when a first pass conflates them cleanly.
- **A frequency claim's direction is exactly as easy to get backwards as a sign, and exactly as easy to miss, because both readings produce a sentence that sounds right** (Team B, 2026-09-13, same pass). "Zero in the overwhelming majority of records" was published; the measured value is **91.8% of records have the field non-zero** — the opposite majority. The underlying mechanism claim (a genuine field, not padding, and ignoring it breaks the walk) was correct throughout; only the "how common is zero" direction was inverted. **A frequency majority claim needs the actual count checked, not just the mechanism** — "usually zero" and "usually non-zero" are both plausible-sounding sentences about the same field, and only one of them is a report of what was measured.
- **A search predicate and an exact oracle inside the same validator can contradict each other, and the search wins silently while the oracle's own value could have caught it** (2026-09-13, the 13.4× zone Mesh-block undercount). `mesh_scan.py` required its g-side search hit to land 16-aligned, then separately required the exact-replay length to match a value the format itself declares — and on 92.7% of real blocks, that declared length only reproduces from the block's *true* (4-aligned) start, proving the 16-alignment requirement was never actually implied by the format at all. **The tell had been sitting in this project's own published numbers for three days**: one section's own count (~11 blocks/zone) and another section's count (2.84 blocks/zone) disagreed by roughly 4×, and nobody reconciled two of this project's own figures against each other. **When a validator combines a search constraint with an exact-value oracle, and the oracle's own declared value depends on where the search landed, that is a circular design that cannot self-detect its own failure — check the two halves against each other, not just each against the data once.**
- **When two statistics agree exactly, ask whether they are reading off the same pointer before treating the agreement as corroboration** (2026-09-13, same investigation, found while explaining an older, now-refuted inline-mode figure). "Stride byte reads 2 in 3,448, matching the g-backed count exactly" was published as mutual confirmation between two independently-computed numbers. They were not independent: both came from the identical wrong header offset, so both failed together on the identical wrong subset of anchors, and an exact numeric match produced by one shared defect is indistinguishable from an exact numeric match produced by a genuinely regular format. **Perfect agreement between two "independent" statistics is itself a fact to be suspicious of, not simply pleased by, until you have checked they don't share a pointer.**
- **A claimed false-positive rate computed from field widths is a claim about the data, not a proof, and it can be off by six orders of magnitude while the conclusion it supports stays correct** (Team B, via this investigation, 2026-09-13). An "~2⁻³²" self-check (a hash-like value repeating at a declared length) looked decisive at 99.6%+ pass rate. Measuring its actual chance rate at random, unconstrained positions in the same file type found **6.98%** — because the file content is full of hash-and-length-shaped records, not the unstructured noise the arithmetic rate assumes. The check remained informative (a real ~14× separation survives), and the conclusion it was used to support was correct — but the *stated* confidence was wrong by a margin large enough to matter elsewhere. **An arithmetic false-positive rate is a hypothesis about the null distribution; measure the null distribution on real data before quoting the arithmetic figure as the actual rate.**
- **"Most-recent-write-wins" replay logic silently collapses a multi-way branch to whichever text comes last, with no signal a branch even existed — and it corrupted an already-PUBLISHED, `CONFIRMED` row, not just an unpublished one** (2026-09-12, streaming budgets). A prior pass's replay tooling read a function containing an `if`/`else` write to the same field and kept only the textually-last assignment, producing a flat, unconditional-looking value that was then published with high confidence. **The defect was invisible from the published number alone** — `0xC00000` looked exactly like a real flat budget, and only re-reading the source (while chasing an unrelated tooling bug) surfaced the second branch. **A replay harness that silently resolves branches by text order rather than by control flow is not a simplification, it is a source of false confidence indistinguishable from a correct extraction until someone reads the original code again.** Generalisable check: whenever a "replay" tool reduces conditional writes to one value, ask specifically whether it detected the branch and chose, or never saw the branch at all — those look identical in the output.
- **A sibling-field lookup that assumes a shared naming convention between related fields will silently drop matches whenever that convention doesn't hold — and the decompiler's own convention (name by type, not by neighbour) is the one to check first** (2026-09-12, streaming budgets). A tool matching a struct's "name" field to its "id" field assumed they'd share a variable-name *prefix*; Ghidra names stack slots by *type*, so two fields of different types describing the same conceptual pair get unrelated names, and the lookup fails silently rather than erroring. This produced several "name unresolved" rows for data that was sitting in the source in plain text the whole time. **When an automated match against decompiled variable names comes back empty, check whether the naming scheme itself supports the assumed relationship before concluding the data isn't there.**
- **A search confined to one error class cannot falsify a hypothesis whose real defect is a different error class — and "we tried several variations" can disguise that the variations never left the class** (2026-09-13, `.anim_pc`/`.ccmesh_pc` skinning — this project's single biggest retraction). A vertex's blend index was published as a direct rig-bone index, and the competing hypothesis — that it addresses a bone-palette table instead — was tested three ways and refuted: constant byte-offset shifts (−4…+4), a reversed array, a reordered pair of arrays. All three variations are inside the same error class, **"the palette array is read at the wrong position."** The real defect was in a different class entirely, **"the palette array is read at the right position but scored against the wrong sign convention"** (the rig→mesh transform used for skinning is a point inversion, `(−x,−y,−z)`, not the rotation `(x,−y,−z)` published elsewhere) — no number of offset/reversal/reorder variations could ever have found that, because none of them touches the sign. The refuting test's own result (`0/87`, *perfect* anti-correlation, reported at the time as "worth reading as a result rather than a failure") was the tell: a genuinely wrong mapping lands near the shuffled-control chance rate (0.50), not at a deterministic 0.00 — and this project's own words said so, one sentence before drawing the wrong conclusion from it. **Before concluding a search of variations refutes a hypothesis, ask what error classes the search actually spans — and if a refuting result is unusually close to 0% or 100% rather than to chance, treat that as a structural signature to explain, not a stronger refutation.** Caught only because a peer's reconciliation attempt named the exact missing variation, and because this session ran its own from-scratch implementation of the corrected test (independently: 171/172) before accepting either side's account. See `spec-rig-format.md` §11.15 for the full account, and `HANDOFF.md` §27.8 for the retracted worked example this replaced.
- **A classifier's "unclassifiable" bucket is a measurement of its input — print its size, because a large one is a claim about the decode, not rows to drop** (2026-09-13, §11.5's re-measurement under the corrected skinning decode; `spec-rig-format.md` §11.5.1). §11.5 classified bone pairs as same-limb or cross-limb from their lowest common ancestor and wrote `if branch is not None and branch >= 3` — silently filing "these two bones have **no** common ancestor at all" under "not cross-limb" and moving on. Under the retracted direct-index reading that bucket held **6,451 of 32,380 pairs (19.9%)**: one in five of the bone pairs the classifier was asked about were anatomically impossible, two bones in disconnected trees, which in these rigs means a parentless helper bone. Under the corrected palette decode the same bucket is **0 of 32,380**. The count was never printed, so nobody saw it — and it would have flagged the wrong blend-index mapping from the file side alone, a full day before the retraction arrived from a peer. **Every filter's reject pile is data about the decode; count it and look at it.** Distinct from the missing-control-group lesson above, and complementary to it: a control tells you the predicate *can* fire, while the reject count tells you whether the predicate was ever asked a well-formed question in the first place.
- **"Who writes field X" and "who writes *through* X" are different predicates, and a pointer that is written once at allocation is invisible to every version of the first** (2026-09-13, the rig skin gather's `source` array). Three independent passes searched for a function that writes a non-null value into `record+0x00` — a call-graph sweep, a wider write-predicate sweep over all 123 accessors of the registry's base pointer, and a structural scan for uncarved code — and all three returned clean, honestly-bounded negatives. **There was nothing to find: the pointer is written exactly three times in the whole binary** (zeroed by the constructor, set once by an allocator, zeroed by the release), and the per-frame data is written *through* it into the pooled block it points at. The productive question was never "who stores to this field" but "who allocates what this field points at" — and that was answered in one step by anchoring on the **pool globals the release path frees through**, rather than on the subsystem's own base-pointer globals that every earlier pass had used as its anchor. **When a field-writer search comes back empty more than once, ask whether the field is a handle rather than a value — and if it is, switch anchors from the field's readers to its allocator's and destructor's own resources** (a pool, a free-list, an arena), which are named by completely different globals. See `spec-rig-format.md` §11.16.3.
- **A widened search predicate can still be one calling convention short, and a `thiscall` is the one it misses** (2026-09-13, same investigation). A pass deliberately widened its predicate to catch a fill happening inside a *callee's* body rather than at the call site — exactly the right instinct — by looking for "the validated pointer **pushed** and passed whole into a further `CALL`." The function it was looking for is a `thiscall` **method** on that very object: the pointer arrives in `ECX` and is never pushed, and the method never references the subsystem's globals at all, so it also sat outside the 123-function set the sweep enumerated. **On 32-bit MSVC-compiled C++, "the pointer is passed to a callee" means `PUSH` *or* `MOV ECX` — a predicate that names only the stack silently excludes every member function of the class you are investigating**, which is precisely where an allocator or a fill loop lives. Related to the existing "a negative result is only as broad as its predicate" lesson, one level down: this is about the predicate's own *encoding* of a concept, not its scope.
- **An instruction scan that walks a function in address order is not walking it in control-flow order, and an early-out's error path physically precedes the success path it would invalidate** (2026-09-13, same investigation). A record-field access map tracked the register holding a validated pointer and dropped it on clobber; in the canonical accessor, the failure arm's `XOR EAX,EAX` sits **two bytes before** the success arm's `MOV EAX,[EAX]`, so tracking died one instruction early and the scan reported that the accessor does not read the field it exists to return. **Caught only because the scan asserted that exact known positive and printed `false`.** The fix that is safe by construction is to stop modelling flow at all — keep the register live to the end of the function, accept the over-reporting, and verify every hit against raw disassembly (which then correctly rejected four of nine candidates as register reuse). **Deliberate over-reporting plus mandatory per-hit verification beats an under-reporting heuristic, because the first fails loudly and the second fails as a clean negative.**

---
- **A document's own framing of an open gap can be the thing keeping it open (2026-09-13, `spec-physics-format.md` §4.4.6).** `.clmesh_pc`'s `+0xb8` gap survived several passes while described as "one more single reference resolved via `thunk_FUN_00e3e590`", by analogy with the slot next to it. Three of that sentence's four load-bearing words were wrong: that function has one call site engine-wide (not eight), sits in a different module, resolves nothing, and is a *parser* one layer **above** the resolver family it had been grouped with. While the question was "what size does the resolution mechanism carry", it had no answer — no resolution was happening.
